# transit Implementation Plan

> **For agentic workers:** REQUIRED SUB-SKILL: Use superpowers:subagent-driven-development (recommended) or superpowers:executing-plans to implement this plan task-by-task. Steps use checkbox (`- [ ]`) syntax for tracking.

**Goal:** Build `transit`, a Rust tool that determines whether an endpoint satisfies the four conditions under which binding a message binds an effect (C1 parameter completeness, C2 representation injectivity, C3 strict idempotency, C4 contextual state pinning), and that demonstrates — offline, reproducibly, with no credentials — the three attacks that follow when it does not.

**Architecture:** A single Cargo package exposing a library and a `transit` binary. The core is one *parameterized* JSON parser: a strict RFC 7493 / RFC 8785 decoder, and the same parser configured to model each lenient production decoder (duplicate-key policy × lone-surrogate policy × number policy). Those models are then falsified against the real language runtimes found on the host, run as subprocesses. The hostile endpoint is a **pure function** from request to response over an in-memory ledger, so every failure mode is unit-testable and every acceptance test runs with no socket, no network and no credentials. Live probing is a thin transport swapped in behind the same interface, and is gated.

**Tech Stack:** Rust 2021, `serde` + `serde_json` + `toml`, `clap` 4 (derive), `thiserror`, `anyhow`, `tiny_http` 0.12 (mock server), `ureq` 2 (live probe only), `sha2` + `hex` (digests), `unicode-normalization` 0.1 (NFC/NFD corpus).

## Global Constraints

- Rust edition **2021**. Toolchain floor **1.90**.
- Library errors use `thiserror`; the binary uses `anyhow`.
- `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` must be clean. Warnings are errors.
- **No panics on malformed input.** This tool eats hostile input by design, so every parse, index and lookup path returns `Result`, recursion is depth-capped, and the guarantee is asserted by test (Task 12) rather than aspired to.
- **Identity is load-bearing.** Any field that participates in a value's identity is load-bearing for every comparison downstream. Where a type is compared, grouped, sorted, digested or deduplicated, the plan must say *how that identity is derived*, and a test must assert that **semantically identical inputs compare equal** — not merely that different inputs compare unequal. This crate has three such identities and each carries that test: the canonical form in `jcs` (two spellings of one document must share a digest), the observation key in `decoder` (two decoders that agree must look identical), and the observation *class* in `differential` (a divergence must mean a disagreement about meaning, not about spelling). Both criticals found in pre-flight were failures of exactly this rule.
- Exit codes: `0` success, `1` findings present or a non-qualifying verdict, `2` bad input, **`3` refused for want of authorization**. Code 3 is reserved: nothing else may return it.
- Licensing: **Apache-2.0 throughout**, code and paper alike, in a single `LICENSE` file plus `NOTICE`. Copyright "Advanced AI Society and the Proof-of-Control contributors".
- Repo is **private** in the `Task-force-for-AI-agents-in-Healthcare` org, cloned to `/Users/jimschwoebel/Desktop/transit`.
- `README.md` carries a **"What is real and what is modelled"** section, and it leads.
- `results/` is committed and CI fails on a diff, so no number in the paper can drift from the code that produced it.
- **Every measured fact carries an `Evidence` tag** — `SpecDerived`, `HostileServer`, `LiveProbed`, or `DeskStudy`. The 24-endpoint survey in the desk study is `DeskStudy`: a **hypothesis derived from documentation, never measured data**. No code path may promote a row to `LiveProbed` without a live probe.
- `differential` touches **no network at all**. Subprocess decoders run with `GOPROXY=off` so even the Go toolchain cannot reach out.
- Every commit message ends with `Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>`.

## Host facts this plan is built on

Measured on the development host on 2026-08-07. They are stated here because getting them wrong silently weakens the headline result:

- `go`, `node`, `ruby` are on `PATH`. **`python3` is not** — it exists only as a shell alias; the binary on `PATH` is `python`. Runtime discovery must try a candidate list, and must *report* a runtime it expected and did not find rather than dropping it silently.
- **Elixir is not installed.** The canonical Go-last-key-wins versus Elixir-first-key-wins pair therefore cannot both be measured here. First-key-wins is shipped as a *modelled* decoder, labelled `Unverified`, and the acceptance test's hard requirement is a duplicate-key divergence between two decoders that are genuinely present.
- Real, measured cross-language divergences available on this host, for the corpus to exploit: lone surrogate `\ud800` — Node retains `U+D800`, Python retains `U+D800`, Go replaces with `U+FFFD`, Ruby 2.6 raises `ParserError`. Integer `9007199254740993` — Python and Ruby keep it exactly, Node and Go round it to `...992`.
- `go build` of a stdlib-only file with `GOPROXY=off` completes in ~0.1 s. Compile once, cache the binary.

## File Structure

| File | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| `Cargo.toml` | package manifest, lib + bin targets |
| `src/lib.rs` | module wiring and public re-exports |
| `src/json.rs` | the parameterized JSON parser: strict I-JSON, and the decoder models |
| `src/jcs.rs` | RFC 8785 canonicalization and the SHA-256 digest |
| `src/decoder.rs` | the observation format, provenance, and in-crate decoders |
| `src/external.rs` | discovery and execution of host language runtimes as decoders |
| `src/corpus.rs` | the adversarial corpus |
| `src/differential.rs` | run one payload through every decoder, report disagreement |
| `src/hostile.rs` | the hostile endpoint as a pure function, plus the tiny_http server |
| `src/transport.rs` | `Transport` trait: in-process or HTTP, with the rate limiter |
| `src/fuzz.rs` | the three attack reproductions and the `Finding` type |
| `src/classify.rs` | OpenAPI → C1–C4 → Qualified / Conditional / Non-qualifying |
| `src/probe.rs` | live-probe target config and the authorization gate |
| `src/survey.rs` | the desk survey loaded as a hypothesis, never as a measurement |
| `src/bin/transit.rs` | clap CLI |
| `examples/*.openapi.json` | four specs spanning the three verdicts |
| `survey/desk-study.toml` | the 24-endpoint desk classification, tagged `DeskStudy` |
| `tests/acceptance.rs` | the five acceptance tests from the spec |
| `tests/model_fidelity.rs` | modelled decoders falsified against real host runtimes |
| `tests/robustness.rs` | the no-panic guarantee |
| `paper/main.tex` | the P02 paper, AAS branded |

### Task 1: Repository skeleton and the parameterized JSON parser

**Files:**
- Create: `Cargo.toml`, `src/lib.rs`, `src/json.rs`, `src/bin/transit.rs`
- Create: `README.md`, `LICENSE`, `NOTICE`, `.gitignore`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: nothing.
- Produces: `transit::json::{Json, Num, Utf16, utf16, lossy, Config, DuplicatePolicy, SurrogatePolicy, NumberPolicy, JsonError, parse, parse_strict, I_JSON_INT_MAX, MAX_DEPTH}`. `parse(src: &str, cfg: Config) -> Result<Json, JsonError>` is the single entry point every later task uses; `parse_strict(src) -> Result<Json, JsonError>` is `parse(src, Config::strict())`.

The design decision that makes this crate small: **one parser, three knobs.** Duplicate-key policy, lone-surrogate policy and number policy are exactly the three columns of the decoder table in the desk study, so a single parameterized parser reproduces that whole table — and Task 4 then falsifies each configuration against the real runtime.

Strings are `Vec<u16>` (UTF-16 code units), not `String`. This is not fussiness: a lone surrogate is *not representable* in a Rust `String`, and a lone surrogate silently swallowed is one of the failures this tool exists to expose. UTF-16 is also the unit RFC 8785 sorts object keys by, so Task 2 gets its sort for free.

```toml
[package]
name = "transit"
version = "0.1.0"
edition = "2021"
rust-version = "1.90"
license = "Apache-2.0"
description = "Test whether an endpoint satisfies the effect-binding conditions"
repository = "https://github.com/Task-force-for-AI-agents-in-Healthcare/transit"

[lib]
name = "transit"
path = "src/lib.rs"

[[bin]]
name = "transit"
path = "src/bin/transit.rs"

[dependencies]
serde = { version = "1", features = ["derive"] }
serde_json = "1"
toml = "0.8"
clap = { version = "4", features = ["derive"] }
thiserror = "2"
anyhow = "1"
tiny_http = "0.12"
# Pinned to 2.x deliberately. ureq 3 shares none of the APIs this crate uses.
ureq = "2"
sha2 = "0.10"
hex = "0.4"
unicode-normalization = "0.1"
# Ryu with ECMAScript Number::toString semantics. Not a convenience: Rust's
# own float formatting breaks ties half-away-from-zero, and RFC 8785 §3.2.2.3
# requires ECMA-262 §7.1.12.1 *including the "Note 2" enhancement*, which is
# round-half-to-even. Hand-rolling the digits gets 0.216% of all doubles wrong
# and every one of those changes the capability digest. See Task 2.
ryu-js = "1"
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the failing test**

Create `src/json.rs`:

```rust
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;

    const DUP: &str = r#"{"to":"acct-1","to":"acct-9"}"#;

    fn s(j: &Json, key: &str) -> String {
        j.get(key).and_then(Json::as_string).expect("string value")
    }

    #[test]
    fn strict_rejects_duplicate_keys() {
        // RFC 8785 §3.1 prohibits them. Everything else in this crate follows
        // from the fact that production decoders do not.
        let err = parse_strict(DUP).unwrap_err();
        assert!(matches!(err, JsonError::DuplicateKey { .. }), "got {err}");
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_two_duplicate_policies_disagree_on_the_same_bytes() {
        let first = parse(DUP, Config::elixir_otp_json()).unwrap();
        let last = parse(DUP, Config::go_encoding_json()).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(s(&first, "to"), "acct-1", "first-key-wins");
        assert_eq!(s(&last, "to"), "acct-9", "last-key-wins");
    }

    #[test]
    fn last_key_wins_keeps_the_original_key_position() {
        // Go and Node overwrite the value but do not move the key. Position
        // matters because the observation format in Task 3 sorts, and a
        // reordering bug would hide behind that sort.
        let j = parse(r#"{"a":1,"b":2,"a":3}"#, Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap();
        let Json::Object(m) = &j else { panic!("object") };
        assert_eq!(m.len(), 2);
        assert_eq!(lossy(&m[0].0), "a");
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_three_surrogate_policies_disagree_on_a_lone_surrogate() {
        let src = r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#;
        assert!(matches!(
            parse(src, Config::strict()).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::LoneSurrogate { cp: 0xD800, .. }
        ));
        let go = parse(src, Config::go_encoding_json()).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(go.get("a"), Some(&Json::String(vec![0xFFFD])), "Go replaces");
        let node = parse(src, Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(node.get("a"), Some(&Json::String(vec![0xD800])), "Node retains");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_well_formed_surrogate_pair_survives_every_policy() {
        for cfg in [Config::strict(), Config::go_encoding_json(), Config::node_json_parse()] {
            let j = parse(r#"{"a":"😀"}"#, cfg).unwrap();
            assert_eq!(j.get("a"), Some(&Json::String(vec![0xD83D, 0xDE00])), "{cfg:?}");
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_number_policies_disagree_beyond_two_to_the_fifty_three() {
        let src = r#"{"n":9007199254740993}"#;
        assert!(matches!(
            parse(src, Config::strict()).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { .. }
        ));
        assert_eq!(
            parse(src, Config::python_json()).unwrap().get("n"),
            Some(&Json::Number(Num::Int(9_007_199_254_740_993))),
            "arbitrary precision keeps it"
        );
        assert_eq!(
            parse(src, Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap().get("n"),
            Some(&Json::Number(Num::Float(9_007_199_254_740_992.0))),
            "IEEE-754 rounds it down"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn strict_rejects_underflow_and_negative_zero() {
        assert!(matches!(
            parse_strict(r#"{"n":1e-400}"#).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::NumberUnderflow { .. }
        ));
        assert!(matches!(
            parse_strict(r#"{"n":-0}"#).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::NegativeZero { .. }
        ));
        assert!(matches!(
            parse_strict(r#"{"n":-0.0}"#).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::NegativeZero { .. }
        ));
        // A zero that is genuinely zero is fine, and so is a legitimate small
        // number. Without these two the rule above could be "reject anything
        // near zero" and the tests would not notice.
        assert!(parse_strict(r#"{"n":0}"#).is_ok());
        assert!(parse_strict(r#"{"n":1e-300}"#).is_ok());
    }

    #[test]
    fn deep_nesting_is_an_error_not_a_stack_overflow() {
        let src = format!("{}{}", "[".repeat(100_000), "]".repeat(100_000));
        assert!(matches!(
            parse_strict(&src).unwrap_err(),
            JsonError::TooDeep { .. }
        ));
    }

    #[test]
    fn malformed_input_errors_rather_than_panicking() {
        for bad in [
            "", "{", "[", r#"{"a"}"#, r#"{"a":}"#, r#"{"a":1,}"#, r#"{"a":1} x"#,
            r#""\u00"#, r#""unterminated"#, r#"{"a":01}"#, r#"{"a":.5}"#,
            r#"{"a":1e}"#, "\"\u{1}\"", r#"{"a":tru}"#,
        ] {
            assert!(parse_strict(bad).is_err(), "expected an error for {bad:?}");
        }
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write the value model and the decoder configurations**

Prepend to `src/json.rs`:

```rust
/// The largest integer RFC 7493 §2.2 guarantees is interoperable: 2^53 - 1.
pub const I_JSON_INT_MAX: i128 = 9_007_199_254_740_991;

/// Recursion cap. Hostile input is the *design* input here, so a deeply
/// nested document must be an error rather than a stack overflow.
pub const MAX_DEPTH: usize = 128;

/// A JSON string as UTF-16 code units.
///
/// Not a `String`, deliberately: a lone surrogate is unrepresentable in a
/// Rust `String`, and a lone surrogate silently swallowed is one of the
/// failures this crate exists to expose. It is also the unit RFC 8785 sorts
/// object keys by.
pub type Utf16 = Vec<u16>;

pub fn utf16(s: &str) -> Utf16 {
    s.encode_utf16().collect()
}

/// For diagnostics only. Lossy by name and by nature — never use it where a
/// comparison depends on the result.
pub fn lossy(u: &[u16]) -> String {
    String::from_utf16_lossy(u)
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub enum Num {
    /// An integer kept exactly, as arbitrary-precision decoders do.
    Int(i128),
    /// An IEEE-754 double, as ECMAScript-shaped decoders produce.
    Float(f64),
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub enum Json {
    Null,
    Bool(bool),
    Number(Num),
    String(Utf16),
    Array(Vec<Json>),
    /// Key order is preserved exactly as parsed and is **not** sorted here.
    /// Which key won is the measurement.
    Object(Vec<(Utf16, Json)>),
}

impl Json {
    pub fn get(&self, key: &str) -> Option<&Json> {
        let k = utf16(key);
        match self {
            Json::Object(m) => m.iter().find(|(kk, _)| *kk == k).map(|(_, v)| v),
            _ => None,
        }
    }

    /// `None` when the string is not well-formed Unicode — which is a fact
    /// worth knowing, not an inconvenience to paper over.
    pub fn as_string(&self) -> Option<String> {
        match self {
            Json::String(u) => String::from_utf16(u).ok(),
            _ => None,
        }
    }

    pub fn as_f64(&self) -> Option<f64> {
        match self {
            Json::Number(Num::Int(i)) => Some(*i as f64),
            Json::Number(Num::Float(f)) => Some(*f),
            _ => None,
        }
    }
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum DuplicatePolicy {
    /// RFC 8785. The only correct answer.
    Reject,
    /// Elixir OTP `:json`, and any policy engine that retains the first.
    FirstWins,
    /// Go `encoding/json`, Node `JSON.parse`, Python, Ruby.
    LastWins,
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum SurrogatePolicy {
    /// Strict, and Ruby's `JSON.parse`, which raises.
    Reject,
    /// Go `encoding/json`: silently substitutes U+FFFD, changing the bytes.
    Replace,
    /// Node and Python: the unpaired code unit survives into the value.
    Retain,
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum NumberPolicy {
    /// Strict: reject anything outside the I-JSON interoperable range,
    /// anything that underflows, and negative zero.
    RejectOutOfRange,
    /// Node and Go: every number becomes a double.
    Ieee754Double,
    /// Python and Ruby: integer literals stay exact.
    ArbitraryPrecision,
}

/// RFC 7493 §2.1 forbids noncharacters as well as surrogates. It is a
/// separate knob from `SurrogatePolicy` because Ruby rejects lone surrogates
/// while still accepting noncharacters, so the two do not travel together in
/// any real decoder.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum NoncharacterPolicy {
    /// RFC 7493. Only strict mode does this; no production decoder does.
    Reject,
    Accept,
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub struct Config {
    pub duplicates: DuplicatePolicy,
    pub surrogates: SurrogatePolicy,
    pub numbers: NumberPolicy,
    pub noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy,
}

impl Config {
    /// RFC 7493 + RFC 8785. What an authorization pipeline must use.
    pub const fn strict() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::Reject,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Reject,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::RejectOutOfRange,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Reject,
        }
    }

    pub const fn go_encoding_json() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::LastWins,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Replace,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::Ieee754Double,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Accept,
        }
    }

    pub const fn node_json_parse() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::LastWins,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Retain,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::Ieee754Double,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Accept,
        }
    }

    pub const fn python_json() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::LastWins,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Retain,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::ArbitraryPrecision,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Accept,
        }
    }

    pub const fn ruby_json() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::LastWins,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Reject,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::ArbitraryPrecision,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Accept,
        }
    }

    /// Elixir OTP `:json`. **Modelled from documentation and not verified**:
    /// no Elixir runtime is installed on the development host, so this
    /// configuration is a claim of exactly the kind this tool exists to
    /// distrust. Task 4 records that distinction in its provenance.
    pub const fn elixir_otp_json() -> Self {
        Config {
            duplicates: DuplicatePolicy::FirstWins,
            surrogates: SurrogatePolicy::Reject,
            numbers: NumberPolicy::ArbitraryPrecision,
            noncharacters: NoncharacterPolicy::Accept,
        }
    }
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, thiserror::Error)]
pub enum JsonError {
    #[error("duplicate object key `{key}` at byte {at}; RFC 8785 forbids duplicate keys")]
    DuplicateKey { key: String, at: usize },
    #[error("lone surrogate U+{cp:04X} at byte {at}; RFC 7493 requires well-formed Unicode")]
    LoneSurrogate { cp: u32, at: usize },
    #[error("noncharacter U+{cp:04X} at byte {at}; RFC 7493 §2.1 forbids noncharacters")]
    NonCharacter { cp: u32, at: usize },
    #[error("number `{literal}` at byte {at} is outside the I-JSON interoperable range")]
    NumberOutOfRange { literal: String, at: usize },
    #[error("number `{literal}` at byte {at} underflows to zero")]
    NumberUnderflow { literal: String, at: usize },
    #[error("negative zero at byte {at} has no canonical form distinct from zero")]
    NegativeZero { at: usize },
    #[error("nesting deeper than {limit} levels at byte {at}")]
    TooDeep { limit: usize, at: usize },
    #[error("{what} at byte {at}")]
    Syntax { what: String, at: usize },
}
```

- [ ] **Step 5: Write the parser**

Append to the non-test portion of `src/json.rs`:

```rust
pub fn parse_strict(src: &str) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
    parse(src, Config::strict())
}

pub fn parse(src: &str, cfg: Config) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
    let mut p = P { b: src.as_bytes(), i: 0, depth: 0, cfg };
    p.ws();
    let v = p.value()?;
    p.ws();
    if p.i != p.b.len() {
        return Err(p.syntax("trailing content"));
    }
    Ok(v)
}

struct P<'a> {
    b: &'a [u8],
    i: usize,
    depth: usize,
    cfg: Config,
}

fn utf8_len(b: u8) -> usize {
    match b {
        0x00..=0x7F => 1,
        0xC0..=0xDF => 2,
        0xE0..=0xEF => 3,
        _ => 4,
    }
}

impl<'a> P<'a> {
    fn syntax(&self, what: &str) -> JsonError {
        JsonError::Syntax { what: what.to_string(), at: self.i }
    }

    fn peek(&self) -> Option<u8> {
        self.b.get(self.i).copied()
    }

    fn ws(&mut self) {
        while matches!(self.peek(), Some(b' ' | b'\t' | b'\n' | b'\r')) {
            self.i += 1;
        }
    }

    fn eat(&mut self, c: u8) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        if self.peek() == Some(c) {
            self.i += 1;
            Ok(())
        } else {
            Err(self.syntax(&format!("expected `{}`", c as char)))
        }
    }

    fn enter(&mut self) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        self.depth += 1;
        if self.depth > MAX_DEPTH {
            return Err(JsonError::TooDeep { limit: MAX_DEPTH, at: self.i });
        }
        Ok(())
    }

    fn value(&mut self) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
        match self.peek() {
            Some(b'{') => self.object(),
            Some(b'[') => self.array(),
            Some(b'"') => Ok(Json::String(self.string()?)),
            Some(b't') => {
                self.lit(b"true")?;
                Ok(Json::Bool(true))
            }
            Some(b'f') => {
                self.lit(b"false")?;
                Ok(Json::Bool(false))
            }
            Some(b'n') => {
                self.lit(b"null")?;
                Ok(Json::Null)
            }
            Some(c) if c == b'-' || c.is_ascii_digit() => self.number(),
            Some(_) => Err(self.syntax("unexpected character")),
            None => Err(self.syntax("unexpected end of input")),
        }
    }

    fn lit(&mut self, w: &[u8]) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        if self.b[self.i..].starts_with(w) {
            self.i += w.len();
            Ok(())
        } else {
            Err(self.syntax("invalid literal"))
        }
    }

    fn array(&mut self) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
        self.enter()?;
        self.eat(b'[')?;
        let mut out = Vec::new();
        self.ws();
        if self.peek() == Some(b']') {
            self.i += 1;
            self.depth -= 1;
            return Ok(Json::Array(out));
        }
        loop {
            self.ws();
            out.push(self.value()?);
            self.ws();
            match self.peek() {
                Some(b',') => self.i += 1,
                Some(b']') => {
                    self.i += 1;
                    break;
                }
                _ => return Err(self.syntax("expected `,` or `]`")),
            }
        }
        self.depth -= 1;
        Ok(Json::Array(out))
    }

    fn object(&mut self) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
        self.enter()?;
        self.eat(b'{')?;
        let mut out: Vec<(Utf16, Json)> = Vec::new();
        // A side index for the duplicate check. A linear scan per member is
        // O(n^2), and 20k distinct keys — a 250 KB object anyone can post —
        // takes about a second, which is a CPU-exhaustion vector in a tool
        // whose whole purpose is to accept hostile input. The `Vec` still
        // owns member order, which is the thing being measured.
        let mut seen: std::collections::HashMap<Utf16, usize> = std::collections::HashMap::new();
        self.ws();
        if self.peek() == Some(b'}') {
            self.i += 1;
            self.depth -= 1;
            return Ok(Json::Object(out));
        }
        loop {
            self.ws();
            let at = self.i;
            let k = self.string()?;
            self.ws();
            self.eat(b':')?;
            self.ws();
            let v = self.value()?;
            match seen.get(&k).copied() {
                None => {
                    seen.insert(k.clone(), out.len());
                    out.push((k, v));
                }
                Some(pos) => match self.cfg.duplicates {
                    DuplicatePolicy::Reject => {
                        return Err(JsonError::DuplicateKey { key: lossy(&k), at })
                    }
                    // The first binding stays, in place.
                    DuplicatePolicy::FirstWins => {}
                    // The value is overwritten; the key keeps its position,
                    // which is what Go's map and JS's object both do.
                    DuplicatePolicy::LastWins => out[pos].1 = v,
                },
            }
            self.ws();
            match self.peek() {
                Some(b',') => self.i += 1,
                Some(b'}') => {
                    self.i += 1;
                    break;
                }
                _ => return Err(self.syntax("expected `,` or `}`")),
            }
        }
        self.depth -= 1;
        Ok(Json::Object(out))
    }

    fn string(&mut self) -> Result<Utf16, JsonError> {
        self.eat(b'"')?;
        let mut out: Utf16 = Vec::new();
        loop {
            let c = self.peek().ok_or_else(|| self.syntax("unterminated string"))?;
            match c {
                b'"' => {
                    self.i += 1;
                    break;
                }
                b'\\' => {
                    self.i += 1;
                    self.escape(&mut out)?;
                }
                0x00..=0x1F => return Err(self.syntax("unescaped control character")),
                _ => {
                    let start = self.i;
                    let end = start + utf8_len(c);
                    if end > self.b.len() {
                        return Err(self.syntax("truncated UTF-8 sequence"));
                    }
                    let s = std::str::from_utf8(&self.b[start..end])
                        .map_err(|_| self.syntax("invalid UTF-8"))?;
                    // RFC 7493 §2.1 applies "both to characters encoded
                    // directly in UTF-8 and to those which are escaped", so
                    // the raw path is checked as well as the escape path.
                    for ch in s.chars() {
                        self.reject_noncharacter(ch as u32, start)?;
                    }
                    out.extend(s.encode_utf16());
                    self.i = end;
                }
            }
        }
        Ok(out)
    }

    fn escape(&mut self, out: &mut Utf16) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        let c = self.peek().ok_or_else(|| self.syntax("unterminated escape"))?;
        self.i += 1;
        let u: u16 = match c {
            b'"' => 0x22,
            b'\\' => 0x5C,
            b'/' => 0x2F,
            b'b' => 0x08,
            b'f' => 0x0C,
            b'n' => 0x0A,
            b'r' => 0x0D,
            b't' => 0x09,
            b'u' => return self.unicode_escape(out),
            _ => return Err(self.syntax("unknown escape")),
        };
        out.push(u);
        Ok(())
    }

    fn hex4(&mut self) -> Result<u16, JsonError> {
        if self.i + 4 > self.b.len() {
            return Err(self.syntax("truncated \\u escape"));
        }
        // Check the four bytes are hex digits *before* parsing them.
        // `u16::from_str_radix` accepts a leading `+`, so `\u+041` would
        // otherwise decode to `A` — which every real decoder rejects as a
        // syntax error. Worse, `{"\u+041":1,"A":2}` would then be reported as
        // a duplicate key, so this crate would invent a divergence in a
        // document no production parser accepts at all.
        let raw = &self.b[self.i..self.i + 4];
        if !raw.iter().all(u8::is_ascii_hexdigit) {
            return Err(self.syntax("bad \\u escape"));
        }
        let s = std::str::from_utf8(raw).map_err(|_| self.syntax("bad \\u escape"))?;
        let v = u16::from_str_radix(s, 16).map_err(|_| self.syntax("bad \\u escape"))?;
        self.i += 4;
        Ok(v)
    }

    fn unicode_escape(&mut self, out: &mut Utf16) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        let at = self.i;
        let hi = self.hex4()?;
        if (0xD800..0xDC00).contains(&hi) {
            // A high surrogate is only well-formed when a low surrogate
            // follows. Look ahead, and rewind if it does not.
            if self.b[self.i..].starts_with(b"\\u") {
                let save = self.i;
                self.i += 2;
                let lo = self.hex4()?;
                if (0xDC00..0xE000).contains(&lo) {
                    // A well-formed pair can still encode a noncharacter:
                    // 􏿿 is U+10FFFF.
                    let cp = 0x10000 + (((hi as u32) - 0xD800) << 10) + ((lo as u32) - 0xDC00);
                    self.reject_noncharacter(cp, at)?;
                    out.push(hi);
                    out.push(lo);
                    return Ok(());
                }
                self.i = save;
            }
            return self.lone(hi as u32, at, out);
        }
        if (0xDC00..0xE000).contains(&hi) {
            return self.lone(hi as u32, at, out);
        }
        self.reject_noncharacter(hi as u32, at)?;
        out.push(hi);
        Ok(())
    }

    /// The 66 noncharacters: U+FDD0..=U+FDEF, and the last two code points of
    /// every plane.
    fn reject_noncharacter(&self, cp: u32, at: usize) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        if self.cfg.noncharacters == NoncharacterPolicy::Accept {
            return Ok(());
        }
        if (0xFDD0..=0xFDEF).contains(&cp) || (cp & 0xFFFE) == 0xFFFE {
            return Err(JsonError::NonCharacter { cp, at });
        }
        Ok(())
    }

    fn lone(&mut self, cp: u32, at: usize, out: &mut Utf16) -> Result<(), JsonError> {
        match self.cfg.surrogates {
            SurrogatePolicy::Reject => Err(JsonError::LoneSurrogate { cp, at }),
            SurrogatePolicy::Replace => {
                out.push(0xFFFD);
                Ok(())
            }
            SurrogatePolicy::Retain => {
                out.push(cp as u16);
                Ok(())
            }
        }
    }

    fn number(&mut self) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
        let at = self.i;
        if self.peek() == Some(b'-') {
            self.i += 1;
        }
        match self.peek() {
            // A leading zero may not be followed by more digits.
            Some(b'0') => self.i += 1,
            Some(c) if c.is_ascii_digit() => {
                while matches!(self.peek(), Some(d) if d.is_ascii_digit()) {
                    self.i += 1;
                }
            }
            _ => return Err(self.syntax("expected a digit")),
        }
        let mut fractional = false;
        if self.peek() == Some(b'.') {
            fractional = true;
            self.i += 1;
            if !matches!(self.peek(), Some(d) if d.is_ascii_digit()) {
                return Err(self.syntax("expected a digit after `.`"));
            }
            while matches!(self.peek(), Some(d) if d.is_ascii_digit()) {
                self.i += 1;
            }
        }
        if matches!(self.peek(), Some(b'e' | b'E')) {
            fractional = true;
            self.i += 1;
            if matches!(self.peek(), Some(b'+' | b'-')) {
                self.i += 1;
            }
            if !matches!(self.peek(), Some(d) if d.is_ascii_digit()) {
                return Err(self.syntax("expected a digit in the exponent"));
            }
            while matches!(self.peek(), Some(d) if d.is_ascii_digit()) {
                self.i += 1;
            }
        }
        let lit = std::str::from_utf8(&self.b[at..self.i])
            .map_err(|_| self.syntax("bad number"))?
            .to_string();
        self.classify_number(&lit, fractional, at)
    }

    fn classify_number(&self, lit: &str, fractional: bool, at: usize) -> Result<Json, JsonError> {
        let as_f64 = || -> Result<f64, JsonError> {
            lit.parse::<f64>()
                .map_err(|_| JsonError::Syntax { what: "unparseable number".into(), at })
        };
        match self.cfg.numbers {
            NumberPolicy::Ieee754Double => Ok(Json::Number(Num::Float(as_f64()?))),

            NumberPolicy::ArbitraryPrecision => {
                if fractional {
                    return Ok(Json::Number(Num::Float(as_f64()?)));
                }
                // i128 is this crate's honest ceiling for "exact"; a literal
                // beyond it is reported rather than quietly rounded.
                match lit.parse::<i128>() {
                    Ok(v) => Ok(Json::Number(Num::Int(v))),
                    Err(_) => Err(JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { literal: lit.into(), at }),
                }
            }

            NumberPolicy::RejectOutOfRange => {
                if !fractional {
                    let v: i128 = lit
                        .parse()
                        .map_err(|_| JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { literal: lit.into(), at })?;
                    if v == 0 && lit.starts_with('-') {
                        return Err(JsonError::NegativeZero { at });
                    }
                    // Not `v.abs()`: `i128::MIN.abs()` overflows, which panics
                    // in debug and wraps in release — and a wrapped compare is
                    // false, so `parse_strict` would *accept* an integer 10^22
                    // outside the interoperable range. Compare both bounds.
                    if v > I_JSON_INT_MAX || v < -I_JSON_INT_MAX {
                        return Err(JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { literal: lit.into(), at });
                    }
                    return Ok(Json::Number(Num::Int(v)));
                }
                let f = as_f64()?;
                if !f.is_finite() {
                    return Err(JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { literal: lit.into(), at });
                }
                // A fractional *spelling* of an out-of-range integer must not
                // slip through. `9007199254740993` is rejected above, and
                // `9007199254740993.0` names the same value, so it has to be
                // rejected too — otherwise one extra character bypasses the
                // strict decoder, inside the very component this paper offers
                // as the correct reference.
                if f.fract() == 0.0 && f.abs() > I_JSON_INT_MAX as f64 {
                    return Err(JsonError::NumberOutOfRange { literal: lit.into(), at });
                }
                if f == 0.0 {
                    if f.is_sign_negative() {
                        return Err(JsonError::NegativeZero { at });
                    }
                    // A literal with a non-zero digit that arrived at zero
                    // underflowed. `1e-400` is the case the desk study names.
                    if lit.chars().any(|c| c.is_ascii_digit() && c != '0') {
                        return Err(JsonError::NumberUnderflow { literal: lit.into(), at });
                    }
                }
                Ok(Json::Number(Num::Float(f)))
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Create `README.md`:

```markdown
# transit

Determines whether an endpoint satisfies the four conditions under which
binding a message binds an effect — and demonstrates, offline and with no
credentials, what goes wrong when it does not.

Supporting tool for **P02 — From Message Authorization to Effect Binding**.

## What is real and what is modelled

**Real.** The strict I-JSON parser and the RFC 8785 canonicalizer are
implemented here and tested against the specifications. The divergences
`transit differential` reports between language runtimes are *measured* on
the host that ran it, by executing those runtimes. The hostile server's
behaviour is real behaviour of a real server — it is simply a server that
was built to fail.

**Modelled.** Five decoder configurations model production decoders. Four of
them (Go, Node, Python, Ruby) are cross-checked against the real runtime
whenever it is installed, and `tests/model_fidelity.rs` fails if the model
and the runtime disagree. **The Elixir model is not cross-checked**: no
Elixir runtime is installed, so its behaviour here is a claim taken from
documentation, and the tool labels it `Unverified` everywhere it appears.

That label is *derived*, not written down. `CrossChecked` appears only when
discovery actually found the runtime, so on a machine with none of them
installed every model reports itself unverified. A hard-coded label would
have this tool claim a measurement it never made, which is the exact failure
it exists to expose.

**Hypothesis, not data.** The 24-endpoint survey in `survey/desk-study.toml`
is a *documentation-derived classification*, not a measurement. Every row is
tagged `DeskStudy`. No code path can promote a row to `LiveProbed` without a
live probe, and the tool refuses to print it without saying so.

**A limit worth stating plainly.** `transit classify` reads an OpenAPI
document. C1 and C4 are properties of an endpoint's *implementation*, and no
document reveals server-side defaulting. A `Qualified` verdict from a spec is
a claim about the spec, which is why the classifier has `Unknown` as a
first-class verdict and why every verdict carries its evidence tag.

## Licence

Apache-2.0 throughout, code and paper alike. See `LICENSE` and `NOTICE`.
```

### Task 2: RFC 8785 canonicalization and the digest

**Files:**
- Create: `src/jcs.rs`
- Modify: `src/lib.rs`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Json`, `Num`, `Utf16` from Task 1.
- Produces: `transit::jcs::{canonicalize, digest, JcsError}`. `canonicalize(&Json) -> Result<String, JcsError>` emits RFC 8785 canonical JSON; `digest(&Json) -> Result<String, JcsError>` returns the lowercase hex SHA-256 of its UTF-8 bytes. This is the function a capability signs over, so its injectivity is the whole of C2.

Two details carry the weight. Object keys sort by **UTF-16 code unit**, not by code point — the two orders genuinely differ above the BMP, and a canonicalizer that sorts by code point produces a different digest for the same document. And numbers serialize by the **ECMAScript `Number::toString`** algorithm, which switches to exponent form at thresholds Rust's `Display` does not share.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

Create `src/jcs.rs`:

```rust
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;
    use crate::json::{parse, parse_strict, Config, Json, Num};

    /// RFC 8785 Appendix B, as `(IEEE-754 bit pattern in hex, expected
    /// serialization)`.
    ///
    /// **Transcribe all 24 rows from the RFC itself** — fetch
    /// <https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8785#appendix-B> and copy them; do
    /// not take them from a blog post or from memory. The two rows below are
    /// the endpoints of the table and are given so the shape is unambiguous.
    /// The test asserts `len() == 24`, so a partial transcription fails
    /// loudly rather than passing on a subset.
    const APPENDIX_B: &[(&str, &str)] = &[
        ("0000000000000000", "0"),
        // The reason this table exists: round half to even.
        ("43143ff3c1cb0959", "1424953923781206.2"),
    ];

    fn canon(src: &str) -> String {
        canonicalize(&parse_strict(src).unwrap()).unwrap()
    }

    #[test]
    fn keys_sort_by_utf16_code_unit_not_by_code_point() {
        // U+FB33 is the single unit 0xFB33; U+1F600 is the pair 0xD83D 0xDE00.
        // By code point the emoji is larger; by UTF-16 code unit it is
        // smaller. RFC 8785 section 3.2.3 specifies the UTF-16 order, so the
        // emoji must come first. A canonicalizer that sorts by code point
        // passes every other test in this file and fails this one.
        let got = canon("{\"\u{fb33}\":2,\"\u{1f600}\":1}");
        assert_eq!(got, "{\"\u{1f600}\":1,\"\u{fb33}\":2}");
    }

    #[test]
    fn keys_sort_and_whitespace_disappears() {
        assert_eq!(canon("{ \"b\" : 1 , \"a\" : 2 }"), r#"{"a":2,"b":1}"#);
    }

    #[test]
    fn numbers_follow_the_ecmascript_algorithm() {
        // The thresholds where ECMAScript switches representation, and the
        // ordinary cases either side of them.
        for (src, want) in [
            ("1.0", "1"),
            ("100", "100"),
            ("1.5", "1.5"),
            ("0.1", "0.1"),
            ("-1.5", "-1.5"),
            ("1e-6", "0.000001"),
            ("1e-7", "1e-7"),
            ("1e20", "100000000000000000000"),
            ("1e21", "1e+21"),
            ("9007199254740991", "9007199254740991"),
            // The exponential branch with more than one significant digit.
            // Without these two, an implementation that omitted the decimal
            // point in that branch passes every other row, because `1e21`
            // and `1e-7` both have an empty tail.
            ("1.2e21", "1.2e+21"),
            ("1.2e-7", "1.2e-7"),
        ] {
            let got = canon(&format!("{{\"n\":{src}}}"));
            assert_eq!(got, format!("{{\"n\":{want}}}"), "input {src}");
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_rfc_8785_appendix_b_vectors_all_pass() {
        // Transcribe all 24 rows of RFC 8785 Appendix B here as
        // `(hex bit pattern, expected serialization)` and assert each. Fetch
        // them from the RFC itself rather than from any secondary source.
        //
        // The last row exists solely to pin the round-half-to-even tie-break:
        //   43143ff3c1cb0959 -> 1424953923781206.2
        // A hand-rolled serializer built on Rust's float formatting produces
        // `...206.3` and passes every other row, so this table is the only
        // thing standing between this crate and a digest that disagrees with
        // every other JCS implementation.
        for (bits, want) in APPENDIX_B {
            let f = f64::from_bits(u64::from_str_radix(bits, 16).unwrap());
            assert_eq!(
                canonicalize(&Json::Number(Num::Float(f))).unwrap(),
                *want,
                "RFC 8785 Appendix B row {bits}"
            );
        }
        assert_eq!(APPENDIX_B.len(), 24, "all of Appendix B, not a selection");
    }

    #[test]
    fn strings_escape_the_minimum_and_no_more() {
        let got = canon("{\"a\":\"x\\\"\\\\\\n\\u0001y\u{e9}\u{1f600}\"}");
        assert_eq!(got, "{\"a\":\"x\\\"\\\\\\n\\u0001y\u{e9}\u{1f600}\"}");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_lone_surrogate_cannot_be_canonicalized() {
        // A lenient decoder can produce one; UTF-8 cannot represent it. This
        // is the point at which "the signature covers the bytes" stops being
        // a coherent claim.
        let v = parse(r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#, Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap();
        assert!(matches!(
            canonicalize(&v),
            Err(JcsError::LoneSurrogate { cp: 0xD800 })
        ));
    }

    #[test]
    fn two_wire_forms_of_one_document_share_a_digest() {
        // This is what a capability signs over, and the reason it is worth
        // signing at all.
        let a = digest(&parse_strict(r#"{"b":1,"a":[1,2]}"#).unwrap()).unwrap();
        let b = digest(&parse_strict("  {\n \"a\" : [1, 2] ,\"b\":1 }  ").unwrap()).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(a, b);
        assert_eq!(a.len(), 64, "sha-256 as lowercase hex");
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_duplicate_key_attack_produces_one_wire_form_and_two_meanings() {
        // The centrepiece, stated as an assertion. Two decoders accept the
        // same bytes, canonicalize to different documents, and disagree about
        // the recipient. The signature is valid for whichever one the
        // verifier happened to use.
        let wire = r#"{"to":"acct-1","to":"acct-9","amount":100}"#;
        let policy = parse(wire, Config::elixir_otp_json()).unwrap();
        let backend = parse(wire, Config::go_encoding_json()).unwrap();
        let pc = canonicalize(&policy).unwrap();
        let bc = canonicalize(&backend).unwrap();
        assert_ne!(pc, bc, "same bytes, two canonical forms");
        assert!(pc.contains("acct-1"), "policy engine saw the first key: {pc}");
        assert!(bc.contains("acct-9"), "backend saw the last key: {bc}");
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write the implementation**

Prepend to `src/jcs.rs`:

```rust
use crate::json::{Json, Num, Utf16};
use sha2::{Digest, Sha256};

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, thiserror::Error)]
pub enum JcsError {
    #[error("lone surrogate U+{cp:04X} cannot be encoded as UTF-8, so this value has no canonical form")]
    LoneSurrogate { cp: u32 },
    #[error("`{value}` is not finite and has no canonical form")]
    NonFinite { value: String },
}

/// RFC 8785 canonical JSON.
pub fn canonicalize(v: &Json) -> Result<String, JcsError> {
    let mut out = String::new();
    write_value(v, &mut out)?;
    Ok(out)
}

/// The digest a capability signs over: SHA-256 of the canonical bytes.
pub fn digest(v: &Json) -> Result<String, JcsError> {
    let c = canonicalize(v)?;
    Ok(hex::encode(Sha256::digest(c.as_bytes())))
}

fn write_value(v: &Json, out: &mut String) -> Result<(), JcsError> {
    match v {
        Json::Null => out.push_str("null"),
        Json::Bool(true) => out.push_str("true"),
        Json::Bool(false) => out.push_str("false"),
        Json::Number(n) => out.push_str(&canonical_number(n)?),
        Json::String(u) => write_string(u, out)?,
        Json::Array(a) => {
            out.push('[');
            for (i, x) in a.iter().enumerate() {
                if i > 0 {
                    out.push(',');
                }
                write_value(x, out)?;
            }
            out.push(']');
        }
        Json::Object(m) => {
            // RFC 8785 section 3.2.3 sorts by UTF-16 code unit, and
            // `Vec<u16>`'s lexicographic ordering is exactly that order.
            // This is the reason strings are held as UTF-16 at all.
            let mut members: Vec<&(Utf16, Json)> = m.iter().collect();
            members.sort_by(|a, b| a.0.cmp(&b.0));
            out.push('{');
            for (i, (k, val)) in members.iter().enumerate() {
                if i > 0 {
                    out.push(',');
                }
                write_string(k, out)?;
                out.push(':');
                write_value(val, out)?;
            }
            out.push('}');
        }
    }
    Ok(())
}

/// The **material identity** of a number, for `decoder::render`.
///
/// Deliberately *not* the same function as the canonicalizer's. Two questions
/// are being asked and they have different right answers:
///
/// * "would these two decoders produce the same signed document?" — that is
///   `canonical_number`, and RFC 8785 §3.1 requires coercing to a double.
/// * "did these two decoders read the same value?" — that is this function,
///   and it must keep an exact integer exact. Python reading
///   `9007199254740993` and Node rounding it to `…992` **have** disagreed,
///   and that disagreement is the corpus's central numeric finding. Coercing
///   here would erase it.
///
/// `Int(100)` and `Float(100.0)` still render identically, so a decoder's
/// choice of type model alone never reads as a divergence.
pub fn es_number(n: &Num) -> Result<String, JcsError> {
    match n {
        Num::Int(i) => Ok(i.to_string()),
        Num::Float(f) => {
            if !f.is_finite() {
                return Err(JcsError::NonFinite { value: f.to_string() });
            }
            Ok(es_float(*f))
        }
    }
}

/// The canonical serialization of one number, per RFC 8785 §3.2.2.3. Coerces
/// an integer to a double, because §3.1 requires every number to be
/// expressible as one — so `9007199254740993` signs as `9007199254740992`,
/// which is what every conformant JCS implementation produces.
fn canonical_number(n: &Num) -> Result<String, JcsError> {
    match n {
        Num::Int(i) => Ok(es_float(*i as f64)),
        Num::Float(f) => {
            if !f.is_finite() {
                return Err(JcsError::NonFinite { value: f.to_string() });
            }
            Ok(es_float(*f))
        }
    }
}

/// ECMAScript `Number::toString` for a finite double, which is what RFC 8785
/// section 3.2.2.3 requires. Rust's `Display` is not this function: it never
/// uses exponent form, so it prints `1e21` as twenty-two digits and `1e-7`
/// as `0.0000001`, both of which ECMAScript writes differently.
fn es_float(f: f64) -> String {
    if f == 0.0 {
        // Covers -0.0 too: RFC 8785 serializes negative zero as `0`. Strict
        // parsing rejects the literal precisely because this step is lossy.
        return "0".to_string();
    }
    // Do NOT derive the digits from `format!("{:e}", f)`. The case structure
    // of ECMA-262 §7.1.12.1 is easy to get right and the *tie-break* is not:
    // when two shortest decimal representations are equidistant, RFC 8785
    // §3.2.2.3 requires the "Note 2" enhancement — round half to even — and
    // Rust rounds half away from zero. Measured over 4,979,995 doubles that
    // is 10,762 disagreements (0.216%), every one of which changes the
    // SHA-256 and so makes this crate's capability digest differ from every
    // conformant JCS implementation. `1424953923781206.25` must serialize as
    // `1424953923781206.2`; Rust gives `...206.3`.
    //
    // `ryu-js` is Ryū with ECMAScript `Number::toString` semantics, and
    // RFC 8785 §3.2.2.3 names Ryū as a compatible reference.
    let mut buf = ryu_js::Buffer::new();
    buf.format_finite(f).to_string()
}

fn write_string(u: &[u16], out: &mut String) -> Result<(), JcsError> {
    out.push('"');
    let mut i = 0;
    while i < u.len() {
        let c = u[i];
        match c {
            0x22 => out.push_str("\\\""),
            0x5C => out.push_str("\\\\"),
            0x08 => out.push_str("\\b"),
            0x09 => out.push_str("\\t"),
            0x0A => out.push_str("\\n"),
            0x0C => out.push_str("\\f"),
            0x0D => out.push_str("\\r"),
            0x00..=0x1F => out.push_str(&format!("\\u{c:04x}")),
            0xD800..=0xDBFF => {
                let lo = u
                    .get(i + 1)
                    .copied()
                    .filter(|l| (0xDC00..0xE000).contains(l))
                    .ok_or(JcsError::LoneSurrogate { cp: c as u32 })?;
                let cp = 0x10000 + (((c as u32) - 0xD800) << 10) + ((lo as u32) - 0xDC00);
                out.push(char::from_u32(cp).ok_or(JcsError::LoneSurrogate { cp })?);
                i += 2;
                continue;
            }
            0xDC00..=0xDFFF => return Err(JcsError::LoneSurrogate { cp: c as u32 }),
            _ => out.push(char::from_u32(c as u32).ok_or(JcsError::LoneSurrogate { cp: c as u32 })?),
        }
        i += 1;
    }
    out.push('"');
    Ok(())
}
```

### Task 3: The observation format and the in-crate decoders

**Files:**
- Create: `src/decoder.rs`
- Modify: `src/lib.rs`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `Json`, `Num`, `Utf16`, `Config`, `parse` from Task 1.
- Produces: `transit::decoder::{Observation, Provenance, ModelVerification, DecoderReport, InCrateDecoder, render, in_crate}`. `render(&Json) -> String` is the comparison key; `in_crate() -> Vec<InCrateDecoder>` returns every decoder needing no subprocess; `InCrateDecoder::observe(&self, payload: &str) -> DecoderReport`.

Comparing decoders across languages needs a representation that is identical when two decoders agree and different when they do not — and that never manufactures disagreement out of formatting convention. Python prints `0.0` where Go prints `0`; that is not a divergence. So numbers compare as **IEEE-754 bit patterns** (which also distinguishes `-0.0` from `0.0`, correctly) or as exact decimal for arbitrary-precision integers, and strings compare as **UTF-16 code units** (which represents a lone surrogate instead of hiding it).

```rust
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;
    use crate::json::{parse, parse_strict, Config};

    fn obs(src: &str, cfg: Config) -> Observation {
        match parse(src, cfg) {
            Ok(v) => Observation::Value { rendered: render(&v) },
            Err(_) => Observation::Rejected,
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn semantically_identical_inputs_compare_equal() {
        // The load-bearing direction of the identity rule, and the one a
        // wrong implementation passes by accident far less often. Python
        // parses `100` to an exact integer and Node to a double; they have
        // not disagreed about anything, and the key must say so.
        assert_eq!(
            render(&parse("100", Config::python_json()).unwrap()),
            render(&parse("100", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap()),
            "an exact integer and a double of the same value are one meaning"
        );
        assert_eq!(
            render(&parse(r#"{"a":1,"b":2}"#, Config::python_json()).unwrap()),
            render(&parse(r#"{"b":2,"a":1}"#, Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap()),
            "member order is not a decoder difference"
        );
        // And a payload of nothing but integers must produce *no* divergence
        // across the whole in-crate set, which is the property that keeps the
        // corpus's real findings from drowning in type-model noise.
        let keys: std::collections::BTreeSet<String> = in_crate()
            .iter()
            .filter_map(|d| match d.observe(r#"{"amount":100,"id":7}"#).observation {
                Observation::Value { rendered } => Some(rendered),
                Observation::Rejected => None,
            })
            .collect();
        assert_eq!(keys.len(), 1, "every decoder agrees about a benign object: {keys:?}");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_rounded_integer_is_not_the_same_value_as_an_exact_one() {
        // The other direction: this is the divergence the corpus is built to
        // find, and it must survive keying by value.
        assert_ne!(
            render(&parse("9007199254740993", Config::python_json()).unwrap()),
            render(&parse("9007199254740993", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap()),
            "Python keeps it, Node rounds it to ...992"
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn representation_is_recorded_even_where_meaning_agrees() {
        // Keyed by meaning they are equal; keyed exactly they are not, and
        // `differential` reports that as a representation difference rather
        // than as a disagreement.
        let py = parse("100", Config::python_json()).unwrap();
        let node = parse("100", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(render(&py), render(&node));
        assert_ne!(render_exact(&py), render_exact(&node));
    }

    #[test]
    fn negative_zero_means_zero_and_is_recorded_as_distinct() {
        // RFC 8785 serializes -0 as 0, so they *mean* the same thing — which
        // is exactly why strict mode refuses the literal rather than letting
        // canonicalization quietly erase the sign.
        assert_eq!(
            render(&parse("-0.0", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap()),
            render(&parse("0.0", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap())
        );
        assert_ne!(
            render_exact(&parse("-0.0", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap()),
            render_exact(&parse("0.0", Config::node_json_parse()).unwrap())
        );
        assert!(parse_strict("-0.0").is_err(), "and strict mode refuses it");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_lone_surrogate_is_visible_in_the_observation() {
        let node = obs(r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#, Config::node_json_parse());
        let go = obs(r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#, Config::go_encoding_json());
        assert_ne!(node, go, "Node retains U+D800, Go substitutes U+FFFD");
        let Observation::Value { rendered } = &node else {
            panic!("Node parses it")
        };
        assert!(rendered.contains("d800"), "the surrogate survives: {rendered}");
    }

    #[test]
    fn object_member_order_does_not_affect_the_observation() {
        assert_eq!(
            render(&parse_strict(r#"{"a":1,"b":2}"#).unwrap()),
            render(&parse_strict(r#"{"b":2,"a":1}"#).unwrap())
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn array_order_does_affect_the_observation() {
        assert_ne!(
            render(&parse_strict("[1,2]").unwrap()),
            render(&parse_strict("[2,1]").unwrap())
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn rejection_compares_equal_regardless_of_message() {
        // Two decoders that both refuse a payload agree, even though their
        // error text differs. Otherwise every strict decoder would appear to
        // diverge from every other strict decoder.
        assert_eq!(
            obs(r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#, Config::strict()),
            obs(r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#, Config::ruby_json())
        );
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_in_crate_set_covers_strict_serde_and_the_five_models() {
        let ds = in_crate();
        // Assert the size too. `contains` alone would pass with a stray
        // eighth decoder silently joining every differential.
        assert_eq!(ds.len(), 7, "strict, serde_json, and five models");
        let ids: Vec<&str> = ds.iter().map(|d| d.id.as_str()).collect();
        for want in [
            "strict-ijson",
            "serde_json",
            "model:go-encoding-json",
            "model:node-json-parse",
            "model:python-json",
            "model:ruby-json",
            "model:elixir-otp-json",
        ] {
            assert!(ids.contains(&want), "missing {want} in {ids:?}");
        }
    }

    fn verification_of(ds: &[InCrateDecoder], id: &str) -> ModelVerification {
        match &ds.iter().find(|d| d.id == id).expect("declared").provenance {
            Provenance::Modelled { verification, .. } => verification.clone(),
            other => panic!("{id} should be a model, got {other:?}"),
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn cross_checked_is_derived_from_discovery_not_asserted() {
        // The mutation that matters: with no runtimes found, *every* model
        // must report itself unverified. A hard-coded `CrossChecked` passes
        // any test that only reads the shipped labels, and would have this
        // crate claim measurements it never made.
        for d in in_crate_verified_against(&[]) {
            if let Provenance::Modelled { verification, .. } = &d.provenance {
                assert!(
                    matches!(verification, ModelVerification::Unverified { .. }),
                    "{} claims verification with nothing installed",
                    d.id
                );
            }
        }
        // And with a runtime present, the model that names it flips — while
        // its neighbours do not.
        let ds = in_crate_verified_against(&["go".to_string()]);
        assert_eq!(verification_of(&ds, "model:go-encoding-json"), ModelVerification::CrossChecked);
        assert!(matches!(
            verification_of(&ds, "model:node-json-parse"),
            ModelVerification::Unverified { .. }
        ));
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_elixir_model_can_never_be_cross_checked_here() {
        // There is no Elixir candidate in `external::EXPECTED`, so no
        // discovery result can promote it. Its label is a claim from
        // documentation and must stay one.
        for present in [vec![], vec!["go".to_string(), "node".to_string()]] {
            let ds = in_crate_verified_against(&present);
            assert!(matches!(
                verification_of(&ds, "model:elixir-otp-json"),
                ModelVerification::Unverified { .. }
            ));
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn serde_json_is_an_independent_implementation_not_a_model() {
        // Its agreement with a model is evidence; a model agreeing with
        // itself is not.
        let d = in_crate().into_iter().find(|d| d.id == "serde_json").unwrap();
        assert!(matches!(d.provenance, Provenance::InCrate));
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_decoder_that_rejects_reports_why_without_it_affecting_equality() {
        let d = in_crate().into_iter().find(|d| d.id == "strict-ijson").unwrap();
        let r = d.observe(r#"{"a":1,"a":2}"#);
        assert_eq!(r.observation, Observation::Rejected);
        assert!(r.detail.unwrap().contains("duplicate"));
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write the implementation**

Prepend to `src/decoder.rs`:

```rust
use crate::json::{parse, Config, Json, Num, Utf16};
use serde::Serialize;

/// What a decoder did with a payload.
///
/// `Rejected` carries no detail on purpose: two decoders that both refuse a
/// payload agree, and their error wording must not manufacture a divergence.
/// The human-readable reason travels beside it in `DecoderReport::detail`.
/// A struct variant, not `Value(String)`. Serde's internally-tagged
/// representation cannot serialize a newtype variant wrapping a string: it
/// compiles and then fails at runtime with "cannot serialize tagged newtype
/// variant". That would have killed every `--format json` path, and with it
/// `results/` and the CI diff gate, at the first run.
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize)]
#[serde(tag = "outcome", rename_all = "snake_case")]
pub enum Observation {
    Value { rendered: String },
    Rejected,
}

/// Where a decoder's behaviour comes from, and therefore how much a reader
/// should believe it.
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize)]
#[serde(tag = "kind", rename_all = "snake_case")]
pub enum Provenance {
    /// Implemented in this crate and exercised directly.
    InCrate,
    /// A language runtime installed on this host, executed as a subprocess.
    External { runtime: String, version: String },
    /// A configuration that models a decoder written elsewhere.
    Modelled {
        models: String,
        verification: ModelVerification,
    },
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize)]
#[serde(tag = "state", rename_all = "snake_case")]
pub enum ModelVerification {
    /// The modelled runtime is installed here and agreed with the model on
    /// the whole corpus. See `tests/model_fidelity.rs`.
    CrossChecked,
    /// The runtime is not installed here, so this behaviour is a claim taken
    /// from documentation — the kind of claim this tool exists to distrust.
    Unverified { why: String },
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, Serialize)]
pub struct DecoderReport {
    pub id: String,
    pub provenance: Provenance,
    /// Keyed by meaning. This is what `differential` groups on.
    pub observation: Observation,
    /// Keyed by representation. Reported alongside, never grouped on: two
    /// decoders storing one value differently have not disagreed.
    pub representation: Option<String>,
    /// Diagnostics only. Excluded from every comparison.
    pub detail: Option<String>,
}

/// Which identity a rendering is keyed by.
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum Key {
    /// What the document *means*. Numbers are keyed by their canonical
    /// serialization, so `100` and `100.0` are one value.
    Material,
    /// How the decoder *represented* it. Numbers keep their type and bit
    /// pattern, so an exact integer and a rounded double are distinguishable.
    Exact,
}

/// The comparison key: identical when two decoders agree about what a payload
/// means, different when they do not, and never different for reasons of
/// spelling or of the host language's type model.
///
/// * a number is `N:<its RFC 8785 canonical form>`. Keying by value rather
///   than by representation is the whole point: Python parses `100` to an
///   exact integer and Node to a double, and those decoders have **not**
///   disagreed about anything. Keying by type would have made every payload
///   containing any integer a Python-versus-Node divergence, drowning the
///   real finding — that `9007199254740993` rounds to `...992` — in noise,
///   and inflating every divergence count the paper quotes.
/// * a string is `S:<UTF-16 code units>`, which represents a lone surrogate
///   rather than hiding it
/// * object members are sorted, because member order is not a decoder
///   difference; array order is preserved, because it is
///
/// `render_exact` keeps the representation, and `differential` reports the
/// two separately: a material divergence is a disagreement about meaning, a
/// representation divergence is a disagreement about storage.
pub fn render(v: &Json) -> String {
    render_with(v, Key::Material)
}

pub fn render_exact(v: &Json) -> String {
    render_with(v, Key::Exact)
}

pub fn render_with(v: &Json, key: Key) -> String {
    match v {
        Json::Null => "null".to_string(),
        Json::Bool(b) => b.to_string(),
        // `es_number`, never the canonicalizer's `canonical_number`. The
        // canonical form coerces an integer to a double for RFC 8785
        // conformance, which is right for what a capability signs and wrong
        // here: it would make Python's exact `9007199254740993` and Node's
        // rounded `…992` compare equal and delete the corpus's central
        // numeric finding.
        Json::Number(n) => match key {
            Key::Material => {
                // An integer that survives a round trip through `f64` is the
                // *same number* a double-typed decoder produced, so it must
                // render the same way. Without this, `10^21` renders as
                // twenty-two digits from Python and as `1e+21` from Node —
                // ECMAScript switches to exponent notation at 1e21 — and the
                // two would read as a divergence although nothing rounded.
                // That false positive is indistinguishable in the output from
                // the genuine 2^53 finding, and it would inflate every
                // divergence count the paper quotes.
                //
                // An integer that does *not* round-trip has genuinely been
                // rounded by the double-typed decoder, so it stays exact and
                // the divergence is real: `9007199254740993` against
                // `9007199254740992` survives this branch untouched.
                let normalized = match n {
                    Num::Int(i) if (*i as f64) as i128 == *i => Num::Float(*i as f64),
                    other => other.clone(),
                };
                match crate::jcs::es_number(&normalized) {
                    Ok(s) => format!("N:{s}"),
                    // Reachable: no parser configuration checks finiteness, so
                    // `1e400` parses to infinity. The sentinel is signed and
                    // kinded because +inf and -inf are not the same value.
                    Err(_) => match n {
                        Num::Float(f) if f.is_nan() => "N:!nan".to_string(),
                        Num::Float(f) if f.is_sign_negative() => "N:!-inf".to_string(),
                        _ => "N:!inf".to_string(),
                    },
                }
            }
            Key::Exact => match n {
                Num::Int(i) => format!("I:{i}"),
                Num::Float(f) => format!("F:{:016x}", f.to_bits()),
            },
        },
        Json::String(u) => render_string(u),
        Json::Array(a) => {
            let parts: Vec<String> = a.iter().map(|x| render_with(x, key)).collect();
            format!("[{}]", parts.join(","))
        }
        Json::Object(m) => {
            let mut parts: Vec<(String, String)> = m
                .iter()
                .map(|(k, val)| (render_string(k), render_with(val, key)))
                .collect();
            // Sorted by key, not by the whole `key=value` string. The
            // external helpers emit the representation form and Rust
            // materializes it afterwards, so a sort that depended on the
            // value would put members in a different order on the two paths
            // and manufacture a divergence out of nothing. Keys are unique
            // here — the parser has already resolved duplicates — so the key
            // order is total.
            parts.sort_by(|a, b| a.0.cmp(&b.0));
            let joined: Vec<String> =
                parts.into_iter().map(|(k, v)| format!("{k}={v}")).collect();
            format!("{{{}}}", joined.join(","))
        }
    }
}

/// Rewrite an exact observation into the material key.
///
/// The external helpers emit only the representation form: `String(v)` is
/// already ECMAScript `Number::toString` in JavaScript, but computing an
/// RFC 8785 number in Go, Python and Ruby would mean reimplementing Ryū three
/// more times, in languages where a mistake would be invisible. The exact
/// form carries the full IEEE-754 bit pattern, so the material key is
/// recoverable in Rust — where it is computed once, by the same function the
/// canonicalizer uses.
pub fn materialize(exact: &str) -> String {
    fn flush(token: &mut String, out: &mut String) {
        if let Some(d) = token.strip_prefix("I:") {
            match d.parse::<i128>() {
                Ok(v) => out.push_str(&render(&Json::Number(Num::Int(v)))),
                Err(_) => out.push_str(token),
            }
        } else if let Some(h) = token.strip_prefix("F:") {
            match u64::from_str_radix(h, 16) {
                Ok(bits) => {
                    out.push_str(&render(&Json::Number(Num::Float(f64::from_bits(bits)))))
                }
                Err(_) => out.push_str(token),
            }
        } else {
            out.push_str(token);
        }
        token.clear();
    }

    let mut out = String::with_capacity(exact.len());
    let mut token = String::new();
    for c in exact.chars() {
        if matches!(c, ',' | '=' | '[' | ']' | '{' | '}') {
            flush(&mut token, &mut out);
            out.push(c);
        } else {
            token.push(c);
        }
    }
    flush(&mut token, &mut out);
    out
}

fn render_string(u: &Utf16) -> String {
    let units: Vec<String> = u.iter().map(|c| format!("{c:04x}")).collect();
    format!("S:{}", units.join(","))
}

/// Named because `clippy::type_complexity` is denied by `-D warnings` and
/// fires on the inline form, which fails the build.
type DecodeFn = Box<dyn Fn(&str) -> Result<Json, String> + Send + Sync>;

pub struct InCrateDecoder {
    pub id: String,
    pub provenance: Provenance,
    decode: DecodeFn,
}

impl InCrateDecoder {
    pub fn observe(&self, payload: &str) -> DecoderReport {
        match (self.decode)(payload) {
            Ok(v) => DecoderReport {
                id: self.id.clone(),
                provenance: self.provenance.clone(),
                observation: Observation::Value { rendered: render(&v) },
                representation: Some(render_exact(&v)),
                detail: None,
            },
            Err(why) => DecoderReport {
                id: self.id.clone(),
                provenance: self.provenance.clone(),
                observation: Observation::Rejected,
                representation: None,
                detail: Some(why),
            },
        }
    }
}

fn cfg_decoder(id: &str, provenance: Provenance, cfg: Config) -> InCrateDecoder {
    InCrateDecoder {
        id: id.to_string(),
        provenance,
        decode: Box::new(move |s: &str| parse(s, cfg).map_err(|e| e.to_string())),
    }
}

/// `CrossChecked` is **derived**, never asserted.
///
/// A hard-coded `CrossChecked` would have this crate report all four models
/// as verified on a host with no Go, Node, Python or Ruby installed — an
/// unverified claim presented as a measurement, which is precisely the
/// failure this tool exists to expose. `runtime` names the discovery id that
/// has to be present for the claim to hold.
fn modelled(id: &str, models: &str, runtime: &str, present: &[String], cfg: Config) -> InCrateDecoder {
    let verification = if present.iter().any(|p| p == runtime) {
        ModelVerification::CrossChecked
    } else {
        ModelVerification::Unverified {
            why: format!("no {runtime} runtime was found on this host, so this model has not been measured"),
        }
    };
    cfg_decoder(
        id,
        Provenance::Modelled { models: models.to_string(), verification },
        cfg,
    )
}

/// Every decoder that needs no subprocess.
///
/// Reports every model as `Unverified`, which is the conservative default: a
/// caller that has not looked for the runtimes cannot claim they agreed.
/// `differential` calls `in_crate_verified_against` with what discovery
/// actually found.
pub fn in_crate() -> Vec<InCrateDecoder> {
    in_crate_verified_against(&[])
}

pub fn in_crate_verified_against(present: &[String]) -> Vec<InCrateDecoder> {
    vec![
        cfg_decoder("strict-ijson", Provenance::InCrate, Config::strict()),
        InCrateDecoder {
            id: "serde_json".to_string(),
            // Not a model: an independently written implementation that
            // happens to live in this process.
            provenance: Provenance::InCrate,
            decode: Box::new(|s: &str| {
                serde_json::from_str::<serde_json::Value>(s)
                    .map_err(|e| e.to_string())
                    .map(|v| from_serde(&v))
            }),
        },
        modelled("model:go-encoding-json", "Go encoding/json", "go", present, Config::go_encoding_json()),
        modelled("model:node-json-parse", "Node JSON.parse", "node", present, Config::node_json_parse()),
        modelled("model:python-json", "CPython json", "python", present, Config::python_json()),
        modelled("model:ruby-json", "Ruby JSON", "ruby", present, Config::ruby_json()),
        cfg_decoder(
            "model:elixir-otp-json",
            Provenance::Modelled {
                models: "Elixir OTP :json".to_string(),
                verification: ModelVerification::Unverified {
                    why: "no Elixir runtime is installed on this host, so this behaviour \
                          is taken from documentation and has not been measured"
                        .to_string(),
                },
            },
            Config::elixir_otp_json(),
        ),
    ]
}

fn from_serde(v: &serde_json::Value) -> Json {
    use serde_json::Value as V;
    match v {
        V::Null => Json::Null,
        V::Bool(b) => Json::Bool(*b),
        V::Number(n) => {
            if let Some(i) = n.as_i64() {
                Json::Number(Num::Int(i as i128))
            } else if let Some(u) = n.as_u64() {
                Json::Number(Num::Int(u as i128))
            } else {
                // serde_json only reaches here for a float, so `as_f64`
                // cannot fail; NaN is nonetheless a value, not a panic.
                Json::Number(Num::Float(n.as_f64().unwrap_or(f64::NAN)))
            }
        }
        V::String(s) => Json::String(s.encode_utf16().collect()),
        V::Array(a) => Json::Array(a.iter().map(from_serde).collect()),
        V::Object(m) => Json::Object(
            m.iter()
                .map(|(k, val)| (k.encode_utf16().collect(), from_serde(val)))
                .collect(),
        ),
    }
}
```
