- [ ] **Step 5: Add the survey and in-crate-only CLI paths**

Add to `differential.rs`, beside `run`:

```rust
/// The corpus through the in-crate decoders only. Every host produces
/// byte-identical output, which is what makes `results/` diff-gateable in
/// CI; the cross-language run is a property of an environment and is
/// recorded separately, with that environment attached.
pub fn run_in_crate_only() -> Run {
    let cases = crate::corpus::cases()
        .into_iter()
        .map(|c| {
            let reports: Vec<DecoderReport> =
                in_crate().iter().map(|d| d.observe(&c.payload)).collect();
            let divergence = classify(&reports);
            CaseReport {
                case: c.name.to_string(),
                condition: Some(c.condition),
                why: c.why.to_string(),
                reports,
                divergence,
            }
        })
        .collect();
    Run { cases, missing: Vec::new() }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 6: Write the no-panic test**

Create `tests/robustness.rs`:

```rust
//! This tool eats hostile input by design, so "no panics on malformed input"
//! has to be a tested guarantee rather than an aspiration. Every entry point
//! that a hostile party can reach is fed malformed input here.

use transit::classify::classify_spec;
use transit::corpus;
use transit::hostile::{handle, FailureModes, Ledger, Request};
use transit::jcs::canonicalize;
use transit::json::{parse, parse_strict, Config};

/// Inputs that are malformed in structurally different ways. Anything that
/// crashes on one of these crashes on a request some stranger can send.
fn hostile_inputs() -> Vec<String> {
    let mut v: Vec<String> = vec![
        String::new(),
        "{".into(),
        "}".into(),
        "[".into(),
        "null".into(),
        "\"unterminated".into(),
        r#"{"a":}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":1,}"#.into(),
        r#"{,}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":01}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":1e}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":-}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"\u"}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"\ud800"}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"\udfff\udfff"}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"\q"}"#.into(),
        "{\"a\":\"\u{1}\"}".into(),
        r#"{"a":1e999999999}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":-1e-999999999}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":99999999999999999999999999999999999999999}"#.into(),
        // i128::MIN. `v.abs()` on this panics in debug and wraps in release,
        // and a wrapped comparison silently accepts it.
        r#"{"n":-170141183460469231731687303715884105728}"#.into(),
        // RFC 7493 noncharacters, escaped and raw, BMP and via a pair.
        r#"{"a":"￾"}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"﷐"}"#.into(),
        r#"{"a":"􏿿"}"#.into(),
        "{\"a\":\"\u{fffe}\"}".into(),
        "\u{feff}{}".into(),
        // Deep nesting, both shapes: the recursion cap has to hold for
        // objects as well as arrays.
        format!("{}{}", "[".repeat(200_000), "]".repeat(200_000)),
        "[".repeat(200_000),
        format!("{}1{}", r#"{"a":"#.repeat(50_000), "}".repeat(50_000)),
    ];
    v.extend(corpus::cases().into_iter().map(|c| c.payload));
    v
}

#[test]
fn every_parser_configuration_survives_every_hostile_input() {
    let configs = [
        Config::strict(),
        Config::go_encoding_json(),
        Config::node_json_parse(),
        Config::python_json(),
        Config::ruby_json(),
        Config::elixir_otp_json(),
    ];
    for src in hostile_inputs() {
        for cfg in configs {
            // The assertion is that this returns at all. A panic fails the
            // test; an `Err` is a correct answer.
            let _ = parse(&src, cfg);
        }
    }
}

#[test]
fn canonicalization_survives_anything_a_lenient_decoder_produces() {
    for src in hostile_inputs() {
        for cfg in [Config::node_json_parse(), Config::go_encoding_json(), Config::python_json()] {
            if let Ok(v) = parse(&src, cfg) {
                let _ = canonicalize(&v);
            }
        }
    }
}

#[test]
fn the_endpoint_answers_every_hostile_body_in_both_configurations() {
    for modes in [FailureModes::all_hostile(), FailureModes::boundable()] {
        let mut state = Ledger::default();
        for src in hostile_inputs() {
            for path in ["/v1/transfer", "/v1/orders", "/v1/state", "/../../etc/passwd", ""] {
                let res = handle(
                    &modes,
                    &mut state,
                    &Request {
                        method: "POST".into(),
                        path: path.into(),
                        headers: vec![("Idempotency-Key".into(), src.chars().take(64).collect())],
                        body: src.clone(),
                    },
                );
                // `>= 200` would be vacuous: every return path already sits in
                // 200..=428, so the assertion would hold against any code the
                // endpoint could emit. Bound it on both sides.
                assert!(
                    (200..500).contains(&res.status),
                    "an answer in range is required, got {}",
                    res.status
                );
            }
        }
    }
}

#[test]
fn the_classifier_survives_documents_that_are_not_specifications() {
    for src in hostile_inputs() {
        let _ = classify_spec(&src);
    }
    // And documents that are JSON but nonsense in OpenAPI's shape.
    for src in [
        r#"{"paths":{"/x":{"post":{"parameters":"not an array"}}}}"#,
        r#"{"paths":{"/x":{"post":{"requestBody":{"content":{"application/json":{"schema":5}}}}}}}"#,
        r#"{"paths":{"/x":5}}"#,
        r#"{"paths":{}}"#,
    ] {
        let _ = classify_spec(src);
    }
}

#[test]
fn strict_parsing_refuses_every_corpus_case_without_crashing() {
    for c in corpus::cases() {
        assert!(parse_strict(&c.payload).is_err(), "case `{}`", c.name);
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 8: Write the regeneration script**

Create `scripts/regen-results.sh`:

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Regenerate every number the paper cites. CI runs this and fails on a diff,
# so a stale figure cannot outlive a code change.
#
# Two kinds of output. The gated files are byte-identical on every host,
# because they come from decoders that ship in this crate. The host file is a
# cross-language measurement, which is a property of an environment: it
# carries that environment inline and is regenerated but not diff-gated.
# What is checked across environments is the claim, in tests/acceptance.rs.
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."
cargo build --release --quiet
BIN=./target/release/transit
mkdir -p results

# --- gated: identical on every host -----------------------------------------
"$BIN" differential --in-crate-only --format json > results/differential-incrate.json

# `classify --format json` already emits an array per spec, so concatenating
# them inside another array would give an array of arrays. Flatten with jq if
# it is present, and otherwise emit the object-per-spec form, which is what
# the paper's table generator reads either way.
{
  echo '{'
  first=1
  for f in examples/*.openapi.json; do
    [ $first -eq 1 ] || echo ','
    first=0
    printf '  "%s": ' "$(basename "$f" .openapi.json)"
    "$BIN" classify --spec "$f" --format json || true
  done
  echo '}'
} > results/classify.json

"$BIN" fuzz --scenario all-hostile --format json > results/fuzz-hostile.json || true
"$BIN" fuzz --scenario boundable --format json > results/fuzz-boundable.json || true
"$BIN" survey survey/desk-study.toml --format json > results/survey-hypothesis.json

# --- ungated: a measurement of this host ------------------------------------
{
  echo '{'
  echo '  "environment": {'
  echo "    \"generated_on\": \"$(uname -sm)\","
  echo "    \"node\": \"$(node --version 2>/dev/null || echo absent)\","
  echo "    \"go\": \"$(go version 2>/dev/null || echo absent)\","
  echo "    \"python\": \"$( (python3 --version || python --version) 2>&1 || echo absent)\","
  echo "    \"ruby\": \"$(ruby --version 2>/dev/null || echo absent)\""
  echo '  },'
  echo '  "run":'
  "$BIN" differential --format json
  echo '}'
} > results/differential-host.json

# --- LaTeX the paper reads directly -----------------------------------------
"$BIN" survey survey/desk-study.toml --format tex > results/survey-hypothesis.tex
{
  echo '% Generated by scripts/regen-results.sh. Do not edit.'
  echo '\begin{tabular}{llr}'
  echo '\toprule'
  echo 'Specification & Verdict & Failing conditions \\'
  echo '\midrule'
  for f in examples/*.openapi.json; do
    name=$(basename "$f" .openapi.json)
    line=$("$BIN" classify --spec "$f" | grep -E '^(GET|POST|PUT|PATCH|DELETE) ' | head -1 || true)
    verdict=$(echo "$line" | sed 's/.*— //')
    echo "\\texttt{${name//_/\\_}} & ${verdict} & \\\\"
  done
  echo '\bottomrule'
  echo '\end{tabular}'
} > results/classify-table.tex

echo "wrote results/"
```

Make it executable and add the `tex` format arm to the `Survey` subcommand, emitting a `tabular` environment whose caption line is `s.banner()` — so even the LaTeX cannot be pasted into the paper without the caveat travelling with it.

```bash
chmod +x scripts/regen-results.sh
```

- [ ] **Step 10: Write `results/README.md`**

```markdown
# results

Generated by `../scripts/regen-results.sh`. Committed on purpose: the paper
cites these numbers, and CI regenerates them and fails on a diff, so a stale
figure cannot outlive a code change. Do not hand-edit.

**Gated.** `differential-incrate.json`, `classify.json`, `fuzz-*.json` and
`survey-hypothesis.json` come from code that ships in this crate and are
byte-identical on every host. CI fails if they drift.

**Not gated.** `differential-host.json` is a cross-language measurement, and
therefore a property of the machine that ran it — the runtime versions are
recorded inside the file. Rerunning it elsewhere legitimately produces
different bytes, so diffing it across environments would fail for the wrong
reason. What is checked everywhere is the *claim*, asserted in
`tests/acceptance.rs`: that the duplicate-key divergence exists among
decoders present on the host.

`survey-hypothesis.json` is a documentation review, not a measurement. Its
first key is the banner that says so.
```

```yaml
        with: { node-version: '22.11.0' }
      - uses: actions/setup-go@v5
        with: { go-version: '1.23.4' }
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: '3.12.7' }
      - uses: ruby/setup-ruby@v1
        with: { ruby-version: '3.3.6' }
      - name: results are current
        run: |
          ./scripts/regen-results.sh
          git diff --exit-code -- results/ ':!results/differential-host.json' || {
            echo "results/ is stale — run ./scripts/regen-results.sh and commit"
            exit 1
          }
```

- [ ] **Step 1: Copy the branded preamble**

Copy the preamble, colour definitions, `\aaimark` command and cover-page TikZ block from `../poc-research-roadmap/ov-poc-standard/paper/main.tex` (lines 1–110) into `paper/main.tex`. Keep the Advanced AI Society palette (`aailime`, `aaiink`, `aaipaper` and the rest) exactly. Change the title to *From Message Authorization to Effect Binding: Conditions, Counterexamples, and a Conformance Harness*, and the strapline to "Signing the bytes is not signing the deed."

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the sections that do not depend on unrun experiments**

Write these in the plain declarative register the existing paper uses — examples before abstractions, explicit statements of what is not known:

- `\section{The Problem}` — **open with the retry ambiguity**, worked all the way through, because it is the cleanest demonstration that the gap is structural rather than an implementation defect: neither branch is correct, and no message-level mechanism can tell an adversarial replay from an infrastructure retry, since that distinction does not exist at the message layer. Then the duplicate-key differential as the adversarial companion. State plainly that every step of each is individually correct and the composition is exploitable, which is precisely why message-level proofs miss it.
- `\section{What Others Have Built}` — complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975; Anderson 1972), naming the assumption that the monitor and the resource share a machine, and observing that capability protocols distribute the reference monitor and inherit a gap the original model defined away. Capability systems (Dennis and Van Horn 1966; Levy; macaroons, Birgisson et al. NDSS 2014). Dolev-Yao, ProVerif and Tamarin as the right tools for the message half **and only that half** — say that a Tamarin proof of the message layer is not evidence about effects. RFC 9110 on idempotency and safety, RFC 8785 and RFC 7493 for canonical form. The confused-deputy problem (Hardy 1988) as the ancestor, and the parser-differential and request-smuggling literature as the same failure in a different suit. ISO 20022 as the strongest existing answer, read closely rather than cited.
- `\section{Writing It Down Precisely}` — the endpoint as a state transformer $E : \Sigma \times W \to \Sigma \times O$ with parsing and canonicalization explicit; message authorization stated separately from effect binding; then C1–C4, each pinned to the code that checks it in `src/classify.rs`.
- `\section{Sufficient, and Not Known To Be Necessary}` — a short section of its own, because a reviewer will test it. The desk study states the restated theorem as an "if and only if" while proving one direction. An endpoint could achieve effect binding by other means — a co-located monitor, or a total function over a fixed state space. Claim sufficiency, state the necessity direction as open, and say why the distinction matters for the standard.
- `\section{How We Checked}` — the harness: one parameterized decoder that reproduces the published decoder table, falsified against the real runtimes; the hostile endpoint as a pure function with individually toggleable failure modes; the boundable configuration as the control. Say explicitly that a check that produces a finding against the hostile configuration and nothing against the boundable one is evidence about the endpoint, while a check that fires against both is evidence about the checker.
- `\section{The Survey We Inherited}` — a short section, because the tool found something in it. The desk study's 24-row table counts 4 Qualified / 7 Conditional / 13 Non-qualifying; its prose reports 4 / 6 / 14, and this paper's own earlier draft repeated those. Square is a Conditional row the 25% figure omits and the 58% figure then absorbs. Report the corrected split, say where it came from, and make the wider point plainly: a convenience sample of 24 endpoints classified from documentation is a taxonomy with illustrative cases, and 54% is not a population estimate any more than 58% was.
- `\section{What We Still Don't Know}` — lead with the checkability problem, which is the methodological crux: C1 and C4 are properties of implementations, not of published interfaces, so absent source access, classification is inference from documentation and black-box probing, and the survey's credibility rests entirely on how that is done. Then verification without cooperation; what replaces binding for the endpoints that cannot qualify; and composition across multi-step workflows, which is untouched and is arguably the question the agent setting actually poses.

## Self-Review

**Spec coverage.** Every `transit` requirement in the design maps to a task. The five components: `hostile-server` (7), `differential` (3, 4, 6), `fuzz` (5, 8, 9), `classify` (10), `probe` (11). The five CLI commands are `serve-hostile` (7), `differential` (6), `fuzz` (8), `classify` (10) and `probe` (11), with `survey` added in Task 12 because the design's "treat the 24-endpoint table as a hypothesis" requirement needs somewhere to live. All five acceptance tests are present and each is written to fail if the claim stops being true: duplicate-key divergence (6), parameter-defaulting bypass (8), retry ambiguity in both branches (9), ISO-qualified against Slack-non-qualifying (10), probe refusing without authorization (11). The shared skeleton, licensing and README are Task 1; `results/` wiring and the no-panic guarantee are Task 12; the paper is Task 13.

**Where this plan is narrower than the design, stated rather than left implicit.** Six places.

1. **`probe` cannot yet probe an arbitrary API.** Its checks address this repository's own routes and parse a hostile-server-shaped response, so against a real endpoint every request 404s. This is the largest gap between design and plan, and the design makes live verification the central empirical contribution. It is not papered over: `FuzzReport::checks_applicable` goes false when the target does not recognise the routes, so an empty finding list reads as "no check applied" rather than "clean". Per-target route and payload configuration is what would close it, and it is not built.
2. **`fuzz --target <url>` refuses anything but loopback**, exiting 2 and pointing at `probe`. It tags findings `HostileServer`, which is only true of a locally served hostile endpoint — hence the refusal rather than a warning. Without it the command would be an ungated path to a third party emitting a false provenance claim, and it deliberately executes a non-idempotent operation twice.
3. **The corpus is a fixed nine-case set**, not combinatorial variants. A fixed corpus is what makes `results/` diff-gateable.
4. **`classify` does not consume probe results.** The design mentions optional probe input; the plumbing to merge a `FuzzReport` into a `Classification` is not built, so a verdict never gets promoted from `SpecDerived` to `LiveProbed`. Conservative, and still a gap.
5. **`differential` takes `--payload <file>`, not `--corpus <dir>`** as the design's CLI table has it, plus `--in-crate-only`, which the table does not.
6. **`classify` resolves only local `$ref`s** (`#/...`). Remote and file references are left unresolved and produce `Unknown`, because resolving them would mean fetching and this command touches no network. Swagger 2.0 is refused outright rather than misread.

**A correction carried back to the source material.** The desk study's 24-row table counts 4 Qualified / 7 Conditional / 13 Non-qualifying. Its prose says 4 / 6 / 14, and P02 inherits those figures — Square is a Conditional row the 25% figure omits and the 58% figure then absorbs. The plan transcribes the table and asserts 4 / 7 / 13, and Task 13 carries the discrepancy into the paper rather than letting it propagate. This is the "no number can drift from the code that produced it" rule finding something on its first application.

**The Elixir substitution, stated plainly.** Acceptance test 1 in the design names "at least two parsers on the host", and the prompt names Go-versus-Elixir as canonical. No Elixir runtime exists on this machine. The test therefore asserts a duplicate-key divergence between `strict-ijson` and `serde_json` — two genuinely present, independently written decoders — and exercises first-key-wins through a model that the tool labels `Unverified` everywhere it appears. This is a real substitution and it is recorded in the plan, in the README, in the acceptance test's own doc comment, and in the paper's method section rather than being quietly absorbed.

**Type consistency.** `Json`, `Num`, `Utf16`, `Config`, `JsonError` (Task 1); `JcsError` (2); `Observation`, `Provenance`, `ModelVerification`, `DecoderReport`, `InCrateDecoder` (3); `External`, `Expected`, `ExternalError` (4); `Case`, `Condition` (5) and `Evidence` (added to the same module in 8); `Divergence`, `CaseReport`, `Run` (6); `FailureModes`, `Ledger`, `Request`, `Response`, `Transfer` (7); `Transport`, `InProcess`, `Http`, `RateLimiter`, `TransportError`, `Finding`, `FuzzReport` (8); `RetryBranch`, `ClientBelief`, `RetryOutcome` (9); `Verdict`, `ConditionVerdict`, `Classification` (10); `Targets`, `Target`, `Authorization`, `GateError`, `ProbeError` (11); `Survey`, `Source`, `Row`, `DeskVerdict`, `RowVerdict` (12). Each is defined once. `Request` and `Response` are defined in `hostile.rs` and re-exported from `transport.rs`, so both module paths name one type. `render(&Json) -> String` keeps its signature from Task 3 onward, and `run_all(&mut T, scenario: &str)` keeps its two-argument form from Task 8 through Task 11.

**Placeholder scan.** No TBDs, and every code step carries real code. Three steps describe content in prose rather than as a literal listing, and each states the constraint that matters: Task 12 Step 4 gives three of the survey's 24 rows as the pattern plus a table of the remaining 21 with their verdicts, since the rest is transcription and the counts test catches a mis-transcription; Task 13 Steps 2 and 4 describe paper prose, where the exact words are a judgment call, and each names what the section must establish.

**One thing a reviewer should check hardest.** The claim that `differential` touches no network is enforced by a source-level string test, which would not catch a network call made through a dependency rather than `ureq`. The Go build is separately pinned with `GOPROXY=off`. If a reviewer can find a third path to the network from that module, the guarantee is weaker than the README says.

## Plan complete

Two execution options:

**1. Subagent-Driven (recommended)** — a fresh subagent per task, reviewed between tasks, fast iteration.

**2. Inline Execution** — tasks executed in this session with batch checkpoints.

## Plan revision — 2026-08-08: the deployable enforcement point

**What changed.** The tool's headline deliverable is now `transit guard`, an
**enforcing reverse proxy** that sits in front of an API and makes the parser
differential unexploitable at the boundary. The research artifacts remain, but
they are now the *evidence for* a deployable thing rather than the deliverable.

**Why this is not a departure.** P02 already argues it. Its restatement of
C7.1.4 ranks the three complete-mediation options and puts **option (c), the
attested enforcement point — "a monitor co-located inside the execution
boundary, such as a sidecar proxy"** — at the top, above direct endpoint
enforcement. Its parser mandate says authorization pipelines must use strict
RFC 8785 decoders that reject duplicate keys, lone surrogates and unbounded
numerics *before* signature verification, and calls that "the cheapest fix in
the paper and probably the one with the largest immediate effect." A proxy that
does exactly that is the paper's own recommendation made deployable.

**What the existing tasks become.** Nothing is discarded, and two things gain a
job they did not have:

* The **hostile endpoint** (Task 7) stops being only a test fixture and becomes
  the *vulnerable upstream* — the thing the proxy is deployed in front of.
* The **attack suite** (Tasks 8–9) becomes the proof — but state the claim
  exactly, because a pre-flight review found the loose version of it false.
  Traced against the three checks in Task 8:

  | attack | condition | through the guard |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | duplicate-key split | C2 | rejected, unconditionally |
  | parameter-defaulting bypass | C1 | rejected **only if** the route names the parameters in `require_parameters` |
  | URI alias collapse | C2 | **passes.** `GET /v1/users/me` has no body and the path is untouched; a proxy cannot know that `me` and `usr_99812` are one resource |

  So: **the guard closes C2 at the boundary unconditionally, C1 where it is
  configured to, and C3/C4 by demanding the headers.** It does not close alias
  collapse, and nothing it could reasonably do would. Task 15 adds the paired
  assertion as a test so the claim is checked rather than asserted, and the
  README and `DEPLOYING.md` say which of the three it stops.
* `classify` (Task 10) gains a second purpose: what it finds an endpoint fails
  is what the guard should be configured to enforce in front of it.

**Execution order**, which is no longer the task numbering:

```
5 → 6 → 7 → 14 (guard) → 8 → 9 → 10 → 11 → 15 (deployment) → 12 → 13
```

The guard is built before the attack suite so the suite can assert *through*
it. New tasks are appended as 14 and 15 rather than renumbered in, because the
existing tasks cross-reference each other by number in a dozen places and a
renumber would silently break those references.

**Enforcement posture, decided:** fail closed, always. A request that violates
a condition the guard cannot fix is rejected with a 4xx and a precise reason.
There is no permissive mode and no silent normalization — the paper's own
position is that un-attested local normalization is the *weak* option (b), so a
proxy that quietly rewrote requests would be implementing the thing the paper
argues against.

---

### Task 14: `transit guard` — the enforcing reverse proxy

**Files:**
- Create: `src/guard.rs`, `examples/guard.toml`
- Modify: `src/lib.rs`, `src/bin/transit.rs`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `json::parse_strict`, `jcs::{canonicalize, digest}`, `corpus::Condition`, `hostile::Request`.
- Produces: `transit::guard::{GuardConfig, Enforcement, RouteRule, Decision, Forward, decide, normalize_target, serve, load_config, GuardError}`. `decide(&GuardConfig, &Request) -> Decision` is the whole policy **and the whole forward plan**; `serve(&GuardConfig)` executes it. CLI: `transit guard --config <guard.toml>`.

**The structural decision, learnt the expensive way.** A pre-flight review of an
earlier draft of this task found five criticals, and **every one of them lived
in the socket adapter** — path normalization, method mapping, header handling.
That draft made `decide` a pure function and then treated the adapter as too
thin to test. It was not thin; it was where the bugs were.

So `decide` now returns the **complete forward plan** — method, normalized
path, query, final header list and canonical body — and `serve` may do nothing
but execute it. Everything an attacker can influence is decided in a pure
function with tests, and the untestable part is one `ureq` call.

**Five things that draft got wrong, kept here as the reasons the code looks
like this.** Each has a test below.

1. **Path confusion.** `starts_with` on the raw target, then handing that raw
   target to a URL parser that removes dot segments. `POST /v1/search/../transfer`
   matched a relaxed `/v1/search` route rule and arrived at the upstream as
   `/v1/transfer` — C3 and C4 unenforced on every mutating endpoint, using the
   shipped example config.
2. **Method rewriting.** A `match` that mapped anything unrecognised to `POST`.
   `HEAD /v1/transfer` with a body was treated as non-mutating by the policy and
   delivered as a `POST` to the upstream.
3. **A forgeable trust signal.** `ureq` does not de-duplicate `x-`-prefixed
   headers, so a client-supplied `X-Transit-Digest` survived alongside the
   guard's own. The proxy reintroduced duplicate-key ambiguity *in the header it
   exists to make trustworthy*.
4. **Hop-by-hop passthrough.** Relaying `Transfer-Encoding` and `Expect` gives
   an attacker a CL/TE desync primitive and a connection-pool desync.
5. **Fail-open on unreadable input.** `let _ = read_to_string(...)` turns a
   non-UTF-8 body into an *empty* body, and the request is forwarded.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing test**

Create `src/guard.rs`:

```rust
#[cfg(test)]
mod tests {
    use super::*;

    fn cfg() -> GuardConfig {
        GuardConfig {
            listen: "127.0.0.1:8080".into(),
            upstream: "http://127.0.0.1:8787".into(),
            enforce: Enforcement::default(),
            route: Vec::new(),
        }
    }

    fn req(method: &str, target: &str, body: &str, headers: &[(&str, &str)]) -> Request {
        let mut h: Vec<(String, String)> =
            headers.iter().map(|(a, b)| (a.to_string(), b.to_string())).collect();
        if !body.is_empty() && !headers.iter().any(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case("content-type")) {
            h.push(("Content-Type".into(), "application/json".into()));
        }
        Request { method: method.into(), path: target.into(), headers: h, body: body.into() }
    }

    fn ok() -> Vec<(&'static str, &'static str)> {
        vec![("Idempotency-Key", "k1"), ("If-Match", "0")]
    }

    fn forwarded(d: Decision) -> Forward {
        match d {
            Decision::Forward(f) => f,
            other => panic!("expected Forward, got {other:?}"),
        }
    }

    fn rejected(d: Decision) -> (u16, Option<Condition>, String) {
        match d {
            Decision::Reject { status, condition, reason } => (status, condition, reason),
            other => panic!("expected Reject, got {other:?}"),
        }
    }

    // --- the happy path, and that it is canonical -------------------------

    #[test]
    fn a_conforming_request_forwards_the_canonical_body_not_the_wire_body() {
        let f = forwarded(decide(&cfg(), &req("POST", "/v1/transfer", r#"{ "to":"acct-9" , "amount":100 }"#, &ok())));
        assert_eq!(f.body, r#"{"amount":100,"to":"acct-9"}"#);
        assert_eq!(f.method, "POST");
        assert_eq!(f.path, "/v1/transfer");
        assert_eq!(f.digest.len(), 64);
    }

    // --- the five criticals from the pre-flight ---------------------------

    #[test]
    fn a_dot_segment_cannot_borrow_a_relaxed_route() {
        // Critical 1. With the shipped config's /v1/search relaxation, this
        // reached /v1/transfer with C3 and C4 unenforced.
        let mut c = cfg();
        c.route.push(RouteRule {
            method: "POST".into(),
            path_prefix: "/v1/search".into(),
            require_idempotency_key: Some(false),
            require_state_precondition: Some(false),
            require_parameters: Vec::new(),
        });
        for target in ["/v1/search/../transfer", "/v1/search/%2e%2e/transfer", "/v1/search/..%2Ftransfer"] {
            let (status, _, _) = rejected(decide(&c, &req("POST", target, r#"{"a":1}"#, &[])));
            assert_eq!(status, 400, "{target} was not refused");
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_route_prefix_matches_only_on_a_segment_boundary() {
        // `/v1/searching` must not borrow `/v1/search`'s relaxation.
        let mut c = cfg();
        c.route.push(RouteRule {
            method: "POST".into(),
            path_prefix: "/v1/search".into(),
            require_idempotency_key: Some(false),
            require_state_precondition: Some(false),
            require_parameters: Vec::new(),
        });
        assert!(matches!(decide(&c, &req("POST", "/v1/search", r#"{"q":"x"}"#, &[])), Decision::Forward(_)));
        let (status, condition, _) = rejected(decide(&c, &req("POST", "/v1/searching", r#"{"q":"x"}"#, &[])));
        assert_eq!(status, 428);
        assert_eq!(condition, Some(Condition::C3));
    }

    #[test]
    fn the_method_reaching_the_upstream_is_the_one_the_policy_judged() {
        // Critical 2. Every allowed method must survive unchanged.
        for m in ["GET", "HEAD", "POST", "PUT", "PATCH", "DELETE", "OPTIONS"] {
            let body = if m == "GET" || m == "HEAD" || m == "OPTIONS" { "" } else { r#"{"a":1}"# };
            let f = forwarded(decide(&cfg(), &req(m, "/v1/thing", body, &ok())));
            assert_eq!(f.method, m);
        }
        // And an unknown method is refused rather than coerced into a POST.
        let (status, _, _) = rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req("FROB", "/v1/thing", "", &ok())));
        assert_eq!(status, 405);
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_head_request_carrying_a_body_is_refused() {
        // Critical 2's delivery vehicle: a body on a method the policy treats
        // as non-mutating.
        let (status, _, _) = rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req("HEAD", "/v1/transfer", r#"{"to":"x"}"#, &ok())));
        assert_eq!(status, 400);
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_client_supplied_digest_header_cannot_survive() {
        // Critical 3. The header the guard exists to make trustworthy must
        // never arrive twice, in any casing.
        let f = forwarded(decide(
            &cfg(),
            &req("POST", "/v1/transfer", r#"{"a":1}"#,
                 &[("Idempotency-Key", "k"), ("If-Match", "0"),
                   ("X-Transit-Digest", "forged"), ("x-transit-digest", "also-forged")]),
        ));
        let n = f.headers.iter().filter(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case("x-transit-digest")).count();
        assert_eq!(n, 1, "exactly one digest header: {:?}", f.headers);
        let (_, v) = f.headers.iter().find(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case("x-transit-digest")).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(*v, f.digest);
        assert_ne!(v, "forged");
    }

    #[test]
    fn hop_by_hop_headers_are_not_relayed() {
        // Critical 4. RFC 9110 §7.6.1, plus Expect and Proxy-*.
        let f = forwarded(decide(
            &cfg(),
            &req("POST", "/v1/transfer", r#"{"a":1}"#,
                 &[("Idempotency-Key", "k"), ("If-Match", "0"),
                   ("Connection", "X-Secret, keep-alive"), ("X-Secret", "leak"),
                   ("Transfer-Encoding", "identity"), ("TE", "trailers"),
                   ("Upgrade", "websocket"), ("Expect", "100-continue"),
                   ("Proxy-Authorization", "Basic x"), ("Keep-Alive", "timeout=5"),
                   ("Content-Length", "9999"), ("Host", "elsewhere")]),
        ));
        for banned in ["connection", "x-secret", "transfer-encoding", "te", "upgrade",
                       "expect", "proxy-authorization", "keep-alive", "content-length", "host"] {
            assert!(
                !f.headers.iter().any(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case(banned)),
                "{banned} was relayed: {:?}", f.headers
            );
        }
        // The ordinary ones survive.
        assert!(f.headers.iter().any(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case("idempotency-key")));
    }

    // --- C1, which the earlier draft did not enforce at all ---------------

    #[test]
    fn a_route_can_require_the_parameters_the_endpoint_would_otherwise_default() {
        // The parameter-defaulting bypass, stopped. Without this the guard
        // forwards `{"to":..,"amount":..}` and the endpoint fills fee_tier
        // from profile state the signature never covered.
        let mut c = cfg();
        c.route.push(RouteRule {
            method: "POST".into(),
            path_prefix: "/v1/transfer".into(),
            require_idempotency_key: None,
            require_state_precondition: None,
            require_parameters: vec!["to".into(), "amount".into(), "fee_tier".into()],
        });
        let (status, condition, reason) =
            rejected(decide(&c, &req("POST", "/v1/transfer", r#"{"to":"acct-9","amount":100}"#, &ok())));
        assert_eq!(status, 422);
        assert_eq!(condition, Some(Condition::C1));
        assert!(reason.contains("fee_tier"), "{reason}");

        // Supplied explicitly, it goes through.
        let body = r#"{"to":"acct-9","amount":100,"fee_tier":"standard"}"#;
        assert!(matches!(decide(&c, &req("POST", "/v1/transfer", body, &ok())), Decision::Forward(_)));
    }

    // --- C3 and C4, on the right methods ---------------------------------

    #[test]
    fn an_idempotency_key_is_demanded_only_where_it_is_meaningful() {
        // POST and PATCH are not idempotent; PUT and DELETE are.
        for m in ["POST", "PATCH"] {
            let (_, condition, _) = rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req(m, "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#, &[("If-Match", "0")])));
            assert_eq!(condition, Some(Condition::C3), "{m}");
        }
        for m in ["PUT", "DELETE"] {
            assert!(matches!(decide(&cfg(), &req(m, "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#, &[("If-Match", "0")])), Decision::Forward(_)), "{m}");
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_state_precondition_is_demanded_on_every_mutating_method() {
        // The earlier draft derived this from idempotency and so exempted
        // PUT and DELETE — the two definitionally state-changing methods.
        for m in ["POST", "PUT", "PATCH", "DELETE"] {
            let (status, condition, _) =
                rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req(m, "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#, &[("Idempotency-Key", "k")])));
            assert_eq!(status, 428, "{m}");
            assert_eq!(condition, Some(Condition::C4), "{m}");
        }
        assert!(matches!(decide(&cfg(), &req("GET", "/v1/t", "", &[])), Decision::Forward(_)));
    }

    // --- content type ----------------------------------------------------

    #[test]
    fn the_content_type_the_guard_judged_is_the_one_the_upstream_receives() {
        // Two Content-Types: the guard read the first, ureq relayed the last.
        let (status, _, _) = rejected(decide(
            &cfg(),
            &req("POST", "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#,
                 &[("Idempotency-Key", "k"), ("If-Match", "0"),
                   ("Content-Type", "application/json"), ("Content-Type", "text/plain")]),
        ));
        assert_eq!(status, 400, "a duplicated Content-Type must be refused");

        // Parameters are allowed; the forwarded type is normalized.
        let f = forwarded(decide(
            &cfg(),
            &req("POST", "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#,
                 &[("Idempotency-Key", "k"), ("If-Match", "0"),
                   ("Content-Type", "Application/JSON; charset=utf-8")]),
        ));
        let (_, ct) = f.headers.iter().find(|(k, _)| k.eq_ignore_ascii_case("content-type")).unwrap();
        assert_eq!(ct, "application/json");
    }

    #[test]
    fn a_media_type_that_merely_looks_like_json_is_refused() {
        for ct in ["application/jsonfoo", "text/plain; x=+json", "text/plain"] {
            let (status, _, _) = rejected(decide(
                &cfg(),
                &req("POST", "/v1/t", r#"{"a":1}"#,
                     &[("Idempotency-Key", "k"), ("If-Match", "0"), ("Content-Type", ct)]),
            ));
            assert_eq!(status, 415, "{ct}");
        }
    }

    // --- fail closed ------------------------------------------------------

    #[test]
    fn every_corpus_payload_is_refused_for_the_right_reason() {
        // Asserting the status and the condition, not merely that something
        // was refused — a `decide` that answered 413 to everything would pass
        // a bare "is it rejected" loop.
        for case in crate::corpus::cases() {
            let (status, condition, _) =
                rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req("POST", "/v1/t", &case.payload, &ok())));
            assert_eq!(status, 400, "case `{}`", case.name);
            assert_eq!(condition, Some(Condition::C2), "case `{}`", case.name);
        }
        // The negative half: a benign object of the same shape goes through,
        // so the loop above distinguishes "strict" from "refuses everything".
        assert!(matches!(
            decide(&cfg(), &req("POST", "/v1/t", r#"{"to":"acct-9","amount":100}"#, &ok())),
            Decision::Forward(_)
        ));
    }

    #[test]
    fn an_oversized_body_is_refused_without_a_condition_label() {
        // 413 and 415 are not representation-injectivity failures, and
        // labelling them C2 would pollute the field the whole tool reports on.
        let big = format!(r#"{{"a":"{}"}}"#, "x".repeat(2 * 1024 * 1024));
        let (status, condition, _) = rejected(decide(&cfg(), &req("POST", "/v1/t", &big, &ok())));
        assert_eq!(status, 413);
        assert_eq!(condition, None);
    }

    #[test]
    fn strict_ingestion_cannot_be_switched_off() {
        // The config field exists so a deployment can be audited. It must not
        // be auditable to a lie.
        let toml = r#"
listen = "127.0.0.1:8080"
upstream = "http://127.0.0.1:8787"
[enforce]
strict_ingestion = false
"#;
        assert!(matches!(parse_config(toml), Err(GuardError::StrictIngestionOff)));
    }

    // --- diagnostics ------------------------------------------------------

    #[test]
    fn a_rejection_reason_cannot_inject_into_a_log_line() {
        // The parser's own errors quote the offending key and numeric
        // literal, both attacker-controlled, and JSON escapes are decoded
        // before the key is stored — so a duplicate key spelled "a\nb" puts a
        // real newline into the reason.
        let (_, _, reason) = rejected(decide(
            &cfg(),
            &req("POST", "/v1/t", "{\"a\\nb\":1,\"a\\nb\":2}", &ok()),
        ));
        assert!(!reason.contains('\n'), "newline reached the reason: {reason:?}");
        assert!(!reason.chars().any(|c| c.is_control()), "control char in {reason:?}");
        assert!(reason.len() <= MAX_REASON_LEN);
    }

    #[test]
    fn malformed_input_never_panics() {
        let deep = "[".repeat(200_000);
        for body in ["", "   ", "{", "\u{feff}{}", &deep] {
            let _ = decide(&cfg(), &req("POST", "/v1/t", body, &ok()));
        }
        for target in ["", "//", "/a//b", "/%", "/%zz", "/../..", "/a?b=%2e%2e"] {
            let _ = decide(&cfg(), &req("GET", target, "", &[]));
        }
    }

    #[test]
    fn normalization_keeps_the_query_and_rejects_traversal() {
        assert_eq!(normalize_target("/v1/t?a=1&b=2").unwrap(), ("/v1/t".to_string(), "a=1&b=2".to_string()));
        assert_eq!(normalize_target("/v1//t/").unwrap().0, "/v1/t/");
        for bad in ["/a/../b", "/a/%2e%2e/b", "/a/./b", "../x", "/a/..%2fb"] {
            assert!(normalize_target(bad).is_err(), "{bad}");
        }
    }
}
```

- [ ] **Step 3: Write the implementation**

Prepend to `src/guard.rs`:

```rust
use crate::corpus::Condition;
use crate::hostile::Request;
use crate::jcs::{canonicalize, digest};
use crate::json::{parse_strict, Json};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
use std::path::Path;

/// RFC 9110 §9.2.2. `PUT` and `DELETE` are idempotent, so a retry needs no
/// key — but they are emphatically mutating, which is a different question.
const IDEMPOTENT_METHODS: &[&str] = &["GET", "HEAD", "PUT", "DELETE", "OPTIONS", "TRACE"];
/// Methods that change state, and therefore need a precondition. Deriving
/// this from idempotency exempted `PUT` and `DELETE` — the two methods that
/// exist to change things.
const MUTATING_METHODS: &[&str] = &["POST", "PUT", "PATCH", "DELETE"];
/// Anything else is refused rather than guessed at.
const ALLOWED_METHODS: &[&str] =
    &["GET", "HEAD", "POST", "PUT", "PATCH", "DELETE", "OPTIONS"];

/// Connection-specific headers an intermediary must not relay (RFC 9110
/// §7.6.1), plus `Expect` and the proxy-authentication pair. Relaying
/// `Transfer-Encoding` hands an attacker a CL/TE desync; relaying `Expect`
/// desynchronises the forwarding client's connection pool.
const HOP_BY_HOP: &[&str] = &[
    "connection", "keep-alive", "te", "trailer", "transfer-encoding", "upgrade",
    "expect", "proxy-authenticate", "proxy-authorization", "proxy-connection",
    "content-length", "host",
];

pub const DEFAULT_MAX_BODY_BYTES: usize = 1024 * 1024;
/// Rejection reasons are read by operators out of logs. Attacker-controlled
/// substrings reach them, so they are bounded and stripped of control
/// characters.
pub const MAX_REASON_LEN: usize = 200;

#[derive(Clone, Debug, Serialize, Deserialize)]
#[serde(deny_unknown_fields)]
pub struct Enforcement {
    #[serde(default = "yes")]
    pub strict_ingestion: bool,
    #[serde(default = "yes")]
    pub require_idempotency_key: bool,
    #[serde(default = "yes")]
    pub require_state_precondition: bool,
    #[serde(default = "default_max_body")]
    pub max_body_bytes: usize,
    #[serde(default = "default_digest_header")]
    pub digest_header: Option<String>,
}

fn yes() -> bool { true }
fn default_max_body() -> usize { DEFAULT_MAX_BODY_BYTES }
fn default_digest_header() -> Option<String> { Some("X-Transit-Digest".to_string()) }

impl Default for Enforcement {
    fn default() -> Self {
        Enforcement {
            strict_ingestion: true,
            require_idempotency_key: true,
            require_state_precondition: true,
            max_body_bytes: DEFAULT_MAX_BODY_BYTES,
            digest_header: default_digest_header(),
        }
    }
}

/// A per-route rule. `require_parameters` is how C1 is enforced: name the
/// fields the endpoint would otherwise fill from its own state, and a request
/// omitting one is refused rather than silently completed downstream.
#[derive(Clone, Debug, Serialize, Deserialize)]
#[serde(deny_unknown_fields)]
pub struct RouteRule {
    pub method: String,
    pub path_prefix: String,
    #[serde(default)]
    pub require_idempotency_key: Option<bool>,
    #[serde(default)]
    pub require_state_precondition: Option<bool>,
    #[serde(default)]
    pub require_parameters: Vec<String>,
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug, Serialize, Deserialize)]
#[serde(deny_unknown_fields)]
pub struct GuardConfig {
    pub listen: String,
    pub upstream: String,
    #[serde(default)]
    pub enforce: Enforcement,
    #[serde(default)]
    pub route: Vec<RouteRule>,
}

#[derive(Debug, thiserror::Error)]
pub enum GuardError {
    #[error("could not read {path}: {source}")]
    Io { path: String, #[source] source: std::io::Error },
    #[error("could not parse {path}: {source}")]
    Parse { path: String, #[source] source: toml::de::Error },
    #[error(
        "`strict_ingestion = false` is refused. It is the one rule this proxy rests on, \
         and a config that could switch it off is a config that can reintroduce the bug \
         it exists to prevent."
    )]
    StrictIngestionOff,
    #[error("could not bind {addr}: {detail}")]
    Bind { addr: String, detail: String },
}

/// Everything the upstream will see. Produced by `decide`, executed verbatim
/// by `serve` — so the request the policy judged is the request that is sent.
#[derive(Clone, Debug)]
pub struct Forward {
    pub method: String,
    pub path: String,
    pub query: String,
    pub headers: Vec<(String, String)>,
    pub body: String,
    pub digest: String,
}

#[derive(Clone, Debug)]
pub enum Decision {
    Forward(Forward),
    /// `condition` is `None` where the refusal is not a C1–C4 failure — an
    /// oversized body or an unusable media type is neither.
    Reject { status: u16, condition: Option<Condition>, reason: String },
}

fn reject(status: u16, condition: Option<Condition>, reason: impl AsRef<str>) -> Decision {
    Decision::Reject { status, condition, reason: sanitize(reason.as_ref()) }
}

/// Bound the length and strip control characters. Parser errors quote the
/// offending key and numeric literal verbatim, both attacker-controlled, and
/// JSON escapes are decoded before the key is stored — so `{"a\nb":1,"a\nb":2}`
/// would otherwise put a real newline into a log line.
fn sanitize(s: &str) -> String {
    let cleaned: String = s
        .chars()
        .map(|c| if c.is_control() { ' ' } else { c })
        .collect();
    cleaned.chars().take(MAX_REASON_LEN).collect()
}

/// Split a request target into a normalized path and its query.
///
/// Traversal is **refused, never resolved**. Resolving it would mean the
/// guard deciding on one path and a URL parser downstream deriving another,
/// which is exactly how a relaxed route rule became a way to reach every
/// mutating endpoint with C3 and C4 switched off.
pub fn normalize_target(target: &str) -> Result<(String, String), &'static str> {
    let (raw_path, query) = match target.split_once('?') {
        Some((p, q)) => (p, q),
        None => (target, ""),
    };
    if raw_path.is_empty() || !raw_path.starts_with('/') {
        return Err("request target must be an absolute path");
    }
    let lowered = raw_path.to_ascii_lowercase();
    if lowered.contains("%2e") || lowered.contains("%2f") || lowered.contains('\\') {
        return Err("encoded path separator or dot segment in the request target");
    }
    let mut out = String::with_capacity(raw_path.len());
    let trailing = raw_path.ends_with('/');
    for seg in raw_path.split('/') {
        if seg.is_empty() {
            continue;
        }
        if seg == "." || seg == ".." {
            return Err("dot segment in the request target");
        }
        out.push('/');
        out.push_str(seg);
    }
    if out.is_empty() {
        out.push('/');
    } else if trailing {
        out.push('/');
    }
    Ok((out, query.to_string()))
}

fn headers_named<'a>(req: &'a Request, name: &str) -> impl Iterator<Item = &'a str> {
    let name = name.to_ascii_lowercase();
    req.headers
        .iter()
        .filter(move |(k, _)| k.to_ascii_lowercase() == name)
        .map(|(_, v)| v.as_str())
}

fn single_header<'a>(req: &'a Request, name: &str) -> Result<Option<&'a str>, ()> {
    let mut it = headers_named(req, name);
    let first = it.next();
    if it.next().is_some() {
        return Err(());
    }
    Ok(first)
}

/// `application/json` or a `+json` structured suffix, parameters ignored.
/// A prefix match accepted `application/jsonfoo`; a `contains("+json")`
/// matched a parameter value.
fn is_json_media_type(value: &str) -> bool {
    let base = value.split(';').next().unwrap_or("").trim().to_ascii_lowercase();
    base == "application/json" || base.ends_with("+json")
}

fn route_matches(rule: &RouteRule, method: &str, path: &str) -> bool {
    if !rule.method.eq_ignore_ascii_case(method) {
        return false;
    }
    let p = rule.path_prefix.trim_end_matches('/');
    // Segment boundary, so `/v1/search` does not lend its relaxation to
    // `/v1/searching`.
    path == p || path.starts_with(&format!("{p}/"))
}

/// The whole policy and the whole forward plan, as a pure function.
pub fn decide(cfg: &GuardConfig, req: &Request) -> Decision {
    let method = req.method.to_ascii_uppercase();
    if !ALLOWED_METHODS.contains(&method.as_str()) {
        return reject(405, None, format!("method {method} is not allowed through this guard"));
    }

    let (path, query) = match normalize_target(&req.path) {
        Ok(v) => v,
        Err(why) => return reject(400, None, why),
    };

    let rule = cfg.route.iter().find(|r| route_matches(r, &method, &path));
    let want_key = rule
        .and_then(|r| r.require_idempotency_key)
        .unwrap_or(cfg.enforce.require_idempotency_key);
    let want_pin = rule
        .and_then(|r| r.require_state_precondition)
        .unwrap_or(cfg.enforce.require_state_precondition);
    let want_params: &[String] = rule.map(|r| r.require_parameters.as_slice()).unwrap_or(&[]);

    if req.body.len() > cfg.enforce.max_body_bytes {
        return reject(
            413,
            None,
            format!("body is {} bytes, over the {}-byte limit", req.body.len(), cfg.enforce.max_body_bytes),
        );
    }

    let has_body = !req.body.trim().is_empty();
    if has_body && matches!(method.as_str(), "GET" | "HEAD" | "OPTIONS") {
        return reject(400, None, format!("{method} carrying a body is refused"));
    }

    let content_type = match single_header(req, "content-type") {
        Ok(v) => v,
        Err(()) => return reject(400, None, "more than one Content-Type header"),
    };

    // C2 — strict ingestion, before anything else looks at the request.
    let parsed = if !has_body {
        None
    } else {
        match content_type {
            Some(ct) if is_json_media_type(ct) => {}
            _ => {
                return reject(
                    415,
                    None,
                    "body is not application/json; this guard forwards only what it can canonicalize",
                )
            }
        }
        match parse_strict(&req.body) {
            Ok(v) => Some(v),
            Err(e) => return reject(400, Some(Condition::C2), e.to_string()),
        }
    };

    // C1 — the parameters the endpoint would otherwise fill from its own state.
    if let Some(value) = &parsed {
        let missing: Vec<&str> = want_params
            .iter()
            .filter(|p| value.get(p).is_none())
            .map(String::as_str)
            .collect();
        if !missing.is_empty() {
            return reject(
                422,
                Some(Condition::C1),
                format!("required parameters absent: {}", missing.join(", ")),
            );
        }
    } else if !want_params.is_empty() && MUTATING_METHODS.contains(&method.as_str()) {
        return reject(422, Some(Condition::C1), "a body is required and none was sent");
    }

    // C3 — only where a retry could not be told from a replay.
    if want_key && !IDEMPOTENT_METHODS.contains(&method.as_str()) {
        match single_header(req, "idempotency-key") {
            Err(()) => return reject(400, None, "more than one Idempotency-Key header"),
            Ok(None) => {
                return reject(
                    428,
                    Some(Condition::C3),
                    format!("{method} is not idempotent and carries no Idempotency-Key"),
                )
            }
            Ok(Some(_)) => {}
        }
    }

    // C4 — on every method that changes state.
    if want_pin && MUTATING_METHODS.contains(&method.as_str()) && single_header(req, "if-match").unwrap_or(None).is_none() {
        return reject(
            428,
            Some(Condition::C4),
            "no If-Match precondition; the effect could depend on state that moved after signing",
        );
    }

    let (body, dg) = match &parsed {
        None => (String::new(), String::new()),
        Some(v) => match (canonicalize(v), digest(v)) {
            (Ok(b), Ok(d)) => (b, d),
            (Err(e), _) | (_, Err(e)) => return reject(400, Some(Condition::C2), e.to_string()),
        },
    };

    // Build the header list the upstream will see. Hop-by-hop headers, every
    // header named by `Connection`, and any client-supplied digest header are
    // all dropped before ours is added.
    let connection_named: Vec<String> = headers_named(req, "connection")
        .flat_map(|v| v.split(',').map(|s| s.trim().to_ascii_lowercase()).collect::<Vec<_>>())
        .collect();
    let digest_name = cfg.enforce.digest_header.clone();
    let mut headers: Vec<(String, String)> = req
        .headers
        .iter()
        .filter(|(k, _)| {
            let lk = k.to_ascii_lowercase();
            !HOP_BY_HOP.contains(&lk.as_str())
                && !connection_named.contains(&lk)
                && !lk.eq_ignore_ascii_case("content-type")
                && digest_name.as_ref().map(|d| !lk.eq_ignore_ascii_case(d)).unwrap_or(true)
        })
        .cloned()
        .collect();
    if !body.is_empty() {
        headers.push(("Content-Type".to_string(), "application/json".to_string()));
    }
    if let (Some(name), false) = (digest_name, dg.is_empty()) {
        headers.push((name, dg.clone()));
    }

    Decision::Forward(Forward { method, path, query, headers, body, digest: dg })
}

pub fn parse_config(text: &str) -> Result<GuardConfig, GuardError> {
    let cfg: GuardConfig = toml::from_str(text).map_err(|source| GuardError::Parse {
        path: "<config>".to_string(),
        source,
    })?;
    if !cfg.enforce.strict_ingestion {
        return Err(GuardError::StrictIngestionOff);
    }
    Ok(cfg)
}

pub fn load_config(path: &Path) -> Result<GuardConfig, GuardError> {
    let text = std::fs::read_to_string(path).map_err(|source| GuardError::Io {
        path: path.display().to_string(),
        source,
    })?;
    parse_config(&text).map_err(|e| match e {
        GuardError::Parse { source, .. } => GuardError::Parse { path: path.display().to_string(), source },
        other => other,
    })
}
```

- [ ] **Step 5: Write `serve`, the adapter that may decide nothing**

Append to `src/guard.rs`. Note what it does *not* do: it does not choose a
method, a path, or a header — `decide` did all of that.

```rust
/// Execute a decision. Every request is handled on its own thread, with a
/// bounded body read and explicit timeouts, because a single slow client
/// must not be able to stop the API behind the proxy.
pub fn serve(cfg: &GuardConfig) -> Result<(), GuardError> {
    let server = tiny_http::Server::http(&cfg.listen).map_err(|e| GuardError::Bind {
        addr: cfg.listen.clone(),
        detail: e.to_string(),
    })?;
    println!(
        "transit guard on http://{} → {}\nfail-closed: strict I-JSON ingestion; \
         idempotency key {}; state precondition {}",
        cfg.listen,
        cfg.upstream,
        if cfg.enforce.require_idempotency_key { "required" } else { "not required" },
        if cfg.enforce.require_state_precondition { "required" } else { "not required" },
    );

    let server = std::sync::Arc::new(server);
    let cfg = std::sync::Arc::new(cfg.clone());
    let agent = ureq::AgentBuilder::new()
        .timeout_connect(std::time::Duration::from_secs(5))
        .timeout(std::time::Duration::from_secs(30))
        .build();

    std::thread::scope(|scope| {
        for _ in 0..8 {
            let server = server.clone();
            let cfg = cfg.clone();
            let agent = agent.clone();
            scope.spawn(move || {
                while let Ok(raw) = server.recv() {
                    handle_one(&cfg, &agent, raw);
                }
            });
        }
    });
    Ok(())
}

fn handle_one(cfg: &GuardConfig, agent: &ureq::Agent, mut raw: tiny_http::Request) {
    // `Connection: upgrade` makes tiny_http hand back the raw socket with no
    // length bound, so a client that then sends nothing would block forever.
    if raw.headers().iter().any(|h| {
        h.field.as_str().as_str().eq_ignore_ascii_case("connection")
            && h.value.as_str().to_ascii_lowercase().contains("upgrade")
    }) {
        let _ = raw.respond(json_response(400, r#"{"error":"upgrade is not supported"}"#));
        return;
    }

    // Bounded read, and a read error is a refusal — never an empty body.
    // `read_to_string` truncates on invalid UTF-8, which would otherwise turn
    // an unreadable request into a well-formed bodyless one and forward it.
    let limit = cfg.enforce.max_body_bytes as u64;
    let mut body = String::new();
    let read = std::io::Read::read_to_string(&mut raw.as_reader().take(limit + 1), &mut body);
    if read.is_err() {
        let _ = raw.respond(json_response(400, r#"{"error":"body is not valid UTF-8"}"#));
        return;
    }
    if body.len() as u64 > limit {
        let _ = raw.respond(json_response(413, r#"{"error":"body too large"}"#));
        return;
    }

    let req = Request {
        method: raw.method().as_str().to_string(),
        path: raw.url().to_string(),
        headers: raw
            .headers()
            .iter()
            .map(|h| (h.field.as_str().as_str().to_string(), h.value.as_str().to_string()))
            .collect(),
        body,
    };

    let response = match decide(cfg, &req) {
        Decision::Reject { status, condition, reason } => {
            eprintln!(
                "REJECT {} {} [{}] {}",
                sanitize(&req.method),
                sanitize(&req.path),
                condition.map(|c| c.title()).unwrap_or("policy"),
                reason
            );
            let body = format!(
                r#"{{"error":"rejected by transit guard","condition":{},"reason":{}}}"#,
                serde_json::to_string(&condition.map(|c| c.title())).unwrap_or_else(|_| "null".into()),
                serde_json::to_string(&reason).unwrap_or_else(|_| "\"\"".into())
            );
            json_response(status, &body)
        }
        Decision::Forward(f) => match forward(cfg, agent, &f) {
            Ok(r) => r,
            Err(detail) => json_response(
                502,
                &format!(
                    r#"{{"error":"upstream unreachable","detail":{}}}"#,
                    serde_json::to_string(&sanitize(&detail)).unwrap_or_else(|_| "\"\"".into())
                ),
            ),
        },
    };
    let _ = raw.respond(response);
}

fn json_response(status: u16, body: &str) -> tiny_http::Response<std::io::Cursor<Vec<u8>>> {
    let mut r = tiny_http::Response::from_string(body).with_status_code(status);
    if let Ok(h) = tiny_http::Header::from_bytes(&b"Content-Type"[..], &b"application/json"[..]) {
        r.add_header(h);
    }
    r
}

/// Relay the upstream's status, headers and body back unchanged. Dropping
/// them would break `Location`, `WWW-Authenticate`, `Set-Cookie` and `ETag` —
/// and C4's own `If-Match` flow depends on the client having seen an `ETag`.
fn forward(
    cfg: &GuardConfig,
    agent: &ureq::Agent,
    f: &Forward,
) -> Result<tiny_http::Response<std::io::Cursor<Vec<u8>>>, String> {
    let url = if f.query.is_empty() {
        format!("{}{}", cfg.upstream.trim_end_matches('/'), f.path)
    } else {
        format!("{}{}?{}", cfg.upstream.trim_end_matches('/'), f.path, f.query)
    };
    // `agent.request` preserves the method verbatim rather than mapping it.
    let mut out = agent.request(&f.method, &url);
    for (k, v) in &f.headers {
        out = out.set(k, v);
    }
    let sent = if f.body.is_empty() { out.call() } else { out.send_string(&f.body) };
    let resp = match sent {
        Ok(r) => r,
        Err(ureq::Error::Status(_, r)) => r,
        Err(e) => return Err(e.to_string()),
    };

    let status = resp.status();
    let names: Vec<String> = resp.headers_names();
    let mut relayed: Vec<(String, String)> = Vec::new();
    for name in names {
        if HOP_BY_HOP.contains(&name.to_ascii_lowercase().as_str()) {
            continue;
        }
        if let Some(v) = resp.header(&name) {
            relayed.push((name, v.to_string()));
        }
    }
    let mut bytes = Vec::new();
    std::io::Read::read_to_end(&mut resp.into_reader(), &mut bytes)
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    let mut out = tiny_http::Response::from_data(bytes).with_status_code(status);
    for (k, v) in relayed {
        if let Ok(h) = tiny_http::Header::from_bytes(k.as_bytes(), v.as_bytes()) {
            out.add_header(h);
        }
    }
    Ok(out)
}
```

Update `src/lib.rs` to add `pub mod guard;`.

- [ ] **Step 6: Ship the example config**

Create `examples/guard.toml`:

```toml
# transit guard — an enforcing reverse proxy.
#
# It strict-ingests every request before the upstream sees it, and forwards the
# CANONICAL form rather than the wire form — so the upstream and whatever
# signed the request cannot be looking at different spellings of one document.
#
# Fail closed. There is no permissive mode, and `strict_ingestion = false` is
# refused at load rather than ignored.
#
# WHAT THIS DOES NOT DO. The guard cannot know what an endpoint defaults, so
# C1 is enforced only where you name the parameters yourself, per route, in
# `require_parameters` below. Point `transit classify` at the endpoint's
# OpenAPI document to find out which parameters those are. Without that list,
# a request omitting a field the endpoint fills from its own state is
# forwarded, and the parameter-defaulting bypass still works.

listen   = "127.0.0.1:8080"
upstream = "http://127.0.0.1:8787"

[enforce]
# Always true. Present so a deployed config can be audited; setting it false
# is an error, not a setting.
strict_ingestion = true

# Demand an Idempotency-Key on methods RFC 9110 does not define as idempotent
# (POST, PATCH), so a network retry can be told from a replay.
require_idempotency_key = true

# Demand an If-Match precondition on every method that changes state
# (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE).
require_state_precondition = true

# A body the guard cannot hold is one it cannot canonicalize.
max_body_bytes = 1048576

# The canonical digest handed to the upstream. Any client-supplied header of
# this name is stripped before ours is added.
digest_header = "X-Transit-Digest"

# C1, per route: the parameters this endpoint would otherwise fill from its
# own state. A request omitting one is refused with 422 rather than completed
# downstream by the server.
[[route]]
method = "POST"
path_prefix = "/v1/transfer"
require_parameters = ["to", "amount", "fee_tier"]

# Read-only endpoints legitimately need neither a key nor a precondition.
# Note there is no `strict_ingestion` key here: the rule the design rests on
# is not route-configurable.
[[route]]
method = "POST"
path_prefix = "/v1/search"
require_idempotency_key = false
require_state_precondition = false
```

- [ ] **Step 7: Wire up the CLI**

Add to `enum Cmd`:

```rust
    /// Run the enforcing reverse proxy in front of an upstream API.
    Guard {
        #[arg(long)]
        config: PathBuf,
    },
```

And to the `match`:

```rust
        Cmd::Guard { config } => {
            let cfg = transit::guard::load_config(&config)?;
            transit::guard::serve(&cfg)?;
            Ok(ExitCode::SUCCESS)
        }
```

- [ ] **Step 8: Demonstrate it end to end**

```bash
cargo run -- serve-hostile --scenario all-hostile      # the vulnerable upstream
cargo run -- guard --config examples/guard.toml        # the enforcement point

# Straight at the endpoint: the attack works.
curl -s -XPOST localhost:8787/v1/transfer -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"to":"acct-legit","amount":100,"to":"acct-attacker"}'

# Through the guard: it never arrives.
curl -si -XPOST localhost:8080/v1/transfer -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H 'Idempotency-Key: k1' -H 'If-Match: 0' \
  -d '{"to":"acct-legit","amount":100,"to":"acct-attacker"}' | head -3

# And the defaulting bypass, which needs the route's require_parameters:
curl -si -XPOST localhost:8080/v1/transfer -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H 'Idempotency-Key: k2' -H 'If-Match: 0' \
  -d '{"to":"acct-9","amount":100}' | head -3
```

Expected: the first pays `acct-attacker`; the second is 400 naming C2; the
third is 422 naming C1 and `fee_tier`; the upstream ledger shows only the
first.

- [ ] **Step 9: Commit**

```bash
git add src/guard.rs src/lib.rs src/bin/transit.rs examples/guard.toml
git commit -m "$(cat <<'GITEOF'
Add `transit guard`, the enforcing reverse proxy

The paper's strongest mediation option is (c), a monitor co-located with
execution, and its cheapest recommendation is strict RFC 8785 ingestion
before signature verification. This is both, deployable, failing closed.

`decide` returns the complete forward plan -- method, normalized path,
final headers, canonical body -- and `serve` executes it without choosing
anything. A pre-flight review of the previous draft found five criticals
and every one lived in the socket adapter: a dot segment let a relaxed
route rule reach every mutating endpoint with C3 and C4 off, unknown
methods were coerced to POST, a client-supplied digest header survived
alongside ours because ureq does not dedupe x- headers, hop-by-hop
headers were relayed, and an unreadable body became an empty one and was
forwarded. Making the plan itself pure is what puts those under test.

C1 is enforced per route via require_parameters, since a proxy cannot
know what an endpoint defaults -- and the config and docs say so rather
than implying four-condition coverage.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>
GITEOF
)"
```

### Task 15: Deployment — container, quickstart, and the demonstration

**Files:**
- Create: `Dockerfile`, `docker-compose.yml`, `docs/DEPLOYING.md`, `scripts/demo.sh`
- Modify: `README.md`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

**Interfaces:**
- Consumes: `transit guard` (Task 14), `transit serve-hostile` (Task 7).
- Produces: a container image, a one-command demonstration, and the operator documentation someone needs to put this in front of a real API.

A tool nobody can deploy is a paper with a build script. This task is what
makes the difference.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the Dockerfile**

```dockerfile
# Build
FROM rust:1.90-slim AS build
WORKDIR /src
COPY Cargo.toml Cargo.lock ./
COPY src ./src
RUN cargo build --release --bin transit

# Run. Distroless-style: no shell, no package manager, nothing to pivot to.
FROM gcr.io/distroless/cc-debian12
COPY --from=build /src/target/release/transit /usr/local/bin/transit
COPY examples/guard.toml /etc/transit/guard.toml
EXPOSE 8080
USER 65532:65532
ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/local/bin/transit"]
CMD ["guard", "--config", "/etc/transit/guard.toml"]
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the demonstration compose file**

`docker-compose.yml` stands up the vulnerable upstream and the guard in front
of it, so the demonstration is one command:

```yaml
services:
  upstream:
    build: .
    command: ["serve-hostile", "--scenario", "all-hostile", "--addr", "0.0.0.0:8787"]
    expose: ["8787"]

  guard:
    build: .
    command: ["guard", "--config", "/etc/transit/guard.toml"]
    environment:
      TRANSIT_UPSTREAM: "http://upstream:8787"
    ports: ["8080:8080"]
    depends_on: [upstream]
```

Make `listen` and `upstream` overridable from the environment in
`load_config` (`TRANSIT_LISTEN`, `TRANSIT_UPSTREAM`), since a container cannot
edit its own config file, and add a test that the override applies.

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `scripts/demo.sh`**

The whole argument in one script: the same payload, twice, with and without
the guard in the path. It must **fail loudly** if the attack does not succeed
against the bare upstream — a demonstration that silently proves nothing is
worse than none.

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# The claim, demonstrated: an attack that works against an endpoint stops
# working when transit guard is in front of it.
set -euo pipefail

PAYLOAD='{"to":"acct-legit","amount":100,"to":"acct-attacker"}'
HDRS=(-H 'Content-Type: application/json' -H 'Idempotency-Key: demo-1' -H 'If-Match: 0')

echo "== straight at the endpoint =="
direct=$(curl -sS -XPOST localhost:8787/v1/transfer "${HDRS[@]}" -d "$PAYLOAD")
echo "$direct"
grep -q 'acct-attacker' <<<"$direct" || {
  echo "FAIL: the attack did not succeed against the bare upstream — the demonstration proves nothing"
  exit 1
}

echo
echo "== through transit guard =="
guarded=$(curl -sS -o /dev/stderr -w '%{http_code}' -XPOST localhost:8080/v1/transfer "${HDRS[@]}" -d "$PAYLOAD" 2>&1)
echo
[[ "$guarded" == *400* ]] || { echo "FAIL: the guard did not reject the payload"; exit 1; }

echo
echo "== the upstream ledger =="
curl -sS localhost:8787/v1/state
echo
echo "One transfer, to acct-attacker, from the unguarded call. The guarded call never arrived."
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write `docs/DEPLOYING.md`**

What an operator actually needs, in this order: the one-command demo; putting
it in front of a real API (`upstream`, `listen`, TLS termination expectations,
and that it is HTTP-only by design because it is meant to run as a sidecar
inside a trust boundary); what each enforcement rule will break and how to find
out before it does (point `classify` at the OpenAPI document first); the
per-route relaxation syntax and the fact that strict ingestion is not among
the relaxable rules; what the 400/405/413/415/422/428 responses mean; and a
plain statement that this is option (c) of C7.1.4.

**Then a section headed "What this does not do", placed before the usage
instructions rather than after them.** In these terms:

* **C2 is closed at the boundary, unconditionally.** Duplicate keys, lone
  surrogates, noncharacters and unbounded numerics cannot reach the upstream,
  and what does reach it is the canonical form — so the upstream and the signer
  cannot be reading two spellings of one document.
* **C1 is closed only where you configure it.** A proxy cannot know what an
  endpoint defaults. Name those parameters per route in `require_parameters`,
  using `transit classify` on the OpenAPI document to find them. **Without that
  list the parameter-defaulting bypass still works.**
* **C3 and C4 are demanded, not verified.** The guard requires an
  `Idempotency-Key` and an `If-Match` to be present. Whether the upstream
  honours them is not something a proxy can check.
* **Alias collapse is not addressed at all.** `/v1/users/me` and
  `/v1/users/usr_99812` are one resource under two names; the guard sees two
  paths, no body, and forwards both.
* **It binds what reaches the upstream, not what the upstream does with it.**
  That is the honest ceiling on any enforcement point outside the process
  performing the effect.

- [ ] **Step 5: Pin the claim with a test, not prose**

Create `tests/guard_blocks_the_attacks.rs`. Each check in `fuzz` gets an
assertion about what the guard does with the same payload — including the one
it does **not** stop, so the test records the real boundary:

```rust
//! The pairing the whole deployment argument rests on: an attack that
//! succeeds against the endpoint directly, run past the guard instead.
//!
//! Alias collapse is asserted to PASS. That is not a bug being tolerated —
//! it is the boundary of what a proxy can do, and pinning it stops the
//! README quietly growing a claim the code does not support.

use transit::corpus;
use transit::guard::{decide, Decision, Enforcement, GuardConfig, RouteRule};
use transit::hostile::Request;

fn cfg_with_required_params() -> GuardConfig {
    GuardConfig {
        listen: "127.0.0.1:0".into(),
        upstream: "http://127.0.0.1:8787".into(),
        enforce: Enforcement::default(),
        route: vec![RouteRule {
            method: "POST".into(),
            path_prefix: "/v1/transfer".into(),
            require_idempotency_key: None,
            require_state_precondition: None,
            require_parameters: vec!["to".into(), "amount".into(), "fee_tier".into()],
        }],
    }
}

fn post(path: &str, body: &str) -> Request {
    Request {
        method: "POST".into(),
        path: path.into(),
        headers: vec![
            ("Content-Type".into(), "application/json".into()),
            ("Idempotency-Key".into(), "k1".into()),
            ("If-Match".into(), "0".into()),
        ],
        body: body.into(),
    }
}

#[test]
fn the_duplicate_key_split_never_reaches_the_upstream() {
    let d = decide(&cfg_with_required_params(),
        &post("/v1/transfer", r#"{"to":"acct-1","amount":100,"fee_tier":"standard","to":"acct-9"}"#));
    assert!(matches!(d, Decision::Reject { condition: Some(corpus::Condition::C2), .. }), "{d:?}");
}

#[test]
fn the_defaulting_bypass_is_stopped_when_the_route_names_the_parameters() {
    let d = decide(&cfg_with_required_params(), &post("/v1/transfer", r#"{"to":"acct-9","amount":100}"#));
    assert!(matches!(d, Decision::Reject { condition: Some(corpus::Condition::C1), .. }), "{d:?}");
}

#[test]
fn the_defaulting_bypass_is_not_stopped_without_that_list() {
    // The honest half. An operator who does not configure require_parameters
    // gets no C1 enforcement, and the docs say so.
    let mut c = cfg_with_required_params();
    c.route.clear();
    let d = decide(&c, &post("/v1/transfer", r#"{"to":"acct-9","amount":100}"#));
    assert!(matches!(d, Decision::Forward(_)), "{d:?}");
}

#[test]
fn alias_collapse_passes_through_and_that_is_the_boundary() {
    let mut r = post("/v1/users/me", "");
    r.method = "GET".into();
    r.headers.clear();
    let d = decide(&cfg_with_required_params(), &r);
    assert!(matches!(d, Decision::Forward(_)),
        "a proxy cannot know that /v1/users/me and /v1/users/usr_99812 are one resource");
}

#[test]
fn every_corpus_payload_is_stopped() {
    for case in corpus::cases() {
        let d = decide(&cfg_with_required_params(), &post("/v1/t", &case.payload));
        assert!(matches!(d, Decision::Reject { .. }), "case `{}` got through: {d:?}", case.name);
    }
}
```

```bash
git commit -m "$(cat <<'GITEOF'
Make the guard deployable, and the claim demonstrable in one command

A container, a compose file that stands the vulnerable upstream and the

shell in it is a worse hole than the one it closes.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>
GITEOF
)"
```
